					<h1 id="firstHeading" class="firstHeading mw-first-heading"><span class="mw-page-title-main">Stephen Harper</span></h1>
							
<div id="p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown mw-portlet mw-portlet-lang"  >
	<input type="checkbox" id="p-lang-btn-checkbox" role="button" aria-haspopup="true" data-event-name="ui.dropdown-p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown-checkbox mw-interlanguage-selector" aria-label="Go to an article in another language. Available in 93 languages"   >
	<label id="p-lang-btn-label" for="p-lang-btn-checkbox" class="vector-dropdown-label cdx-button cdx-button--fake-button cdx-button--fake-button--enabled cdx-button--weight-quiet cdx-button--action-progressive mw-portlet-lang-heading-93" aria-hidden="true"  ><span class="vector-icon mw-ui-icon-language-progressive mw-ui-icon-wikimedia-language-progressive"></span>

<span class="vector-dropdown-label-text">93 languages</span>
	</label>
	<div class="vector-dropdown-content">

		<div class="vector-menu-content">
			
			<ul class="vector-menu-content-list">

<hr /></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Harper_sidebar" title="Template:Harper sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Harper_sidebar" title="Template talk:Harper sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Harper_sidebar" title="Special:EditPage/Template:Harper sidebar"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Stephen Joseph Harper</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">CC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Alberta_Order_of_Excellence" title="Alberta Order of Excellence">AOE</a></span> (born April 30, 1959) is a Canadian politician who served as the 22nd <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> from 2006 to 2015. Harper is the first and only prime minister to come from the modern-day <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Conservative Party of Canada">Conservative Party of Canada</a>, serving as the party's first <a href="/wiki/List_of_Canadian_conservative_leaders" title="List of Canadian conservative leaders">leader</a> from 2004 to 2015.
</p><p>Harper studied economics, earning a bachelor's degree in 1985 and a master's degree in 1991 at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Calgary" title="University of Calgary">University of Calgary</a>. He was one of the founders of the <a href="/wiki/Reform_Party_of_Canada" title="Reform Party of Canada">Reform Party of Canada</a> and was first elected in <a href="/wiki/1993_Canadian_federal_election" title="1993 Canadian federal election">1993</a> in <a href="/wiki/Calgary_West" title="Calgary West">Calgary West</a>. He did not seek re-election in the <a href="/wiki/1997_Canadian_federal_election" title="1997 Canadian federal election">1997 federal election</a>, instead joining and later leading the <a href="/wiki/National_Citizens_Coalition" title="National Citizens Coalition">National Citizens Coalition</a>, a conservative lobbyist group. In <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Alliance_leadership_elections#2002_leadership_election" title="Canadian Alliance leadership elections">2002</a>, he succeeded <a href="/wiki/Stockwell_Day" title="Stockwell Day">Stockwell Day</a> as leader of the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Alliance" title="Canadian Alliance">Canadian Alliance</a>, the successor to the Reform Party, and returned to parliament as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">leader of the Official Opposition</a>. In 2003, Harper negotiated the merger of the Canadian Alliance with the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservative Party of Canada</a> to form the Conservative Party of Canada and was elected as the party's first leader in <a href="/wiki/2004_Conservative_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="2004 Conservative Party of Canada leadership election">March 2004</a>. In the <a href="/wiki/2004_Canadian_federal_election" title="2004 Canadian federal election">2004 federal election</a>, the new party lost its first election to the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal Party</a> led by <a href="/wiki/Paul_Martin" title="Paul Martin">Paul Martin</a>.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/2006_Canadian_federal_election" title="2006 Canadian federal election">2006 federal election</a> resulted in a <a href="/wiki/Minority_governments_in_Canada" title="Minority governments in Canada">minority government</a> led by the Conservative Party with Harper becoming prime minister of Canada. During his first term, Harper confronted the <a href="/wiki/In_and_Out_scandal" title="In and Out scandal">In and Out scandal</a>, reduced the <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_services_tax_(Canada)" title="Goods and services tax (Canada)">goods and services tax</a> to five percent, and passed the <i><a href="/wiki/Federal_Accountability_Act" title="Federal Accountability Act">Federal Accountability Act</a></i>, the <a href="/wiki/Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois_nation_motion" title="Québécois nation motion">Québécois nation motion</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Veterans%27_Bill_of_Rights" title="Veterans&#39; Bill of Rights">Veterans' Bill of Rights</a>. After the <a href="/wiki/2008_Canadian_federal_election" title="2008 Canadian federal election">2008 federal election</a>, in which the Conservatives won a larger minority, Harper <a href="/wiki/Prorogation_in_Canada" title="Prorogation in Canada">prorogued Parliament</a> to defeat a <a href="/wiki/2008%E2%80%932009_Canadian_parliamentary_dispute" title="2008–2009 Canadian parliamentary dispute">non-confidence motion by a potential coalition of opposition parties</a>, passed the <a href="/wiki/2009_Canadian_federal_budget" title="2009 Canadian federal budget">Economic Action Plan</a> of major personal income tax cuts and infrastructure investments in response to the <a href="/wiki/Great_Recession" title="Great Recession">Great Recession</a>, introduced the <a href="/wiki/Tax-free_savings_account" title="Tax-free savings account">tax-free savings account</a>, and ordered <a href="/wiki/Operation_Mobile" title="Operation Mobile">military intervention</a> during the <a href="/wiki/First_Libyan_Civil_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First Libyan Civil War">First Libyan Civil War</a>. In March 2011, a <a href="/wiki/No-confidence_vote" class="mw-redirect" title="No-confidence vote">no-confidence vote</a> found his government to be in <a href="/wiki/Contempt_of_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Contempt of Parliament">contempt of Parliament</a>, triggering <a href="/wiki/2011_Canadian_federal_election" title="2011 Canadian federal election">a federal election</a> in which the Conservatives won a <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority government</a>. During his third term, Harper <a href="/wiki/Canada_and_the_Kyoto_Protocol" title="Canada and the Kyoto Protocol">withdrew Canada from the Kyoto Protocol</a>, launched <a href="/wiki/Operation_Impact" title="Operation Impact">Operation Impact</a> in opposition to <a href="/wiki/ISIL" class="mw-redirect" title="ISIL">ISIL</a>, repealed the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Firearms_Registry" title="Canadian Firearms Registry">long-gun registry</a>, passed the <i><a href="/wiki/Anti-terrorism_Act,_2015" title="Anti-terrorism Act, 2015">Anti-terrorism Act, 2015</a></i>, launched <a href="/wiki/Canada%27s_Global_Markets_Action_Plan" title="Canada&#39;s Global Markets Action Plan">Canada's Global Markets Action Plan</a>, and grappled with controversies surrounding the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Senate_expenses_scandal" title="Canadian Senate expenses scandal">Canadian Senate expenses scandal</a> and the <a href="/wiki/2011_Canadian_federal_election_voter_suppression_scandal" title="2011 Canadian federal election voter suppression scandal">Robocall scandal</a>.
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/2015_Canadian_federal_election" title="2015 Canadian federal election">2015 federal election</a>, the Conservative Party lost power to the Liberal Party led by <a href="/wiki/Justin_Trudeau" title="Justin Trudeau">Justin Trudeau</a>. Harper officially stepped down as party leader on October 19, 2015, and resigned his seat on August 26, 2016. Since then, Harper has taken on a number of international business and leadership roles, founding a global consulting firm, appearing in US and British media, and being elected leader of the <a href="/wiki/International_Democrat_Union" class="mw-redirect" title="International Democrat Union">International Democrat Union</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span></h2>
<p>Harper was born and raised in <a href="/wiki/Leaside" title="Leaside">Leaside</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> a neighbourhood of <a href="/wiki/Toronto" title="Toronto">Toronto</a>, Ontario, the first of three sons of Margaret (née Johnston) and Joseph Harris Harper, an accountant at <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Oil" title="Imperial Oil">Imperial Oil</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> The Harper family traces its ancestral roots back to <a href="/wiki/Yorkshire" title="Yorkshire">Yorkshire</a>, England, with Christopher Harper having emigrated to <a href="/wiki/Nova_Scotia" title="Nova Scotia">Nova Scotia</a> in 1784, where he later served as <a href="/wiki/Justice_of_the_peace" title="Justice of the peace">justice of the peace</a> in the area that is now <a href="/wiki/New_Brunswick" title="New Brunswick">New Brunswick</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Globe_And_Mail_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Globe_And_Mail-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper attended Northlea Public School and, later, <a href="/wiki/John_G._Althouse_Middle_School" title="John G. Althouse Middle School">John G. Althouse Middle School</a> and <a href="/wiki/Richview_Collegiate_Institute" title="Richview Collegiate Institute">Richview Collegiate Institute</a>, both in <a href="/wiki/Etobicoke" title="Etobicoke">Etobicoke</a>, Toronto. He graduated from high school in 1978, and was a member of Richview Collegiate's team on <i><a href="/wiki/Reach_for_the_Top" title="Reach for the Top">Reach for the Top</a></i>, a televised academic quiz show for high school students.<sup id="cite_ref-ReachForTheTop_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReachForTheTop-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Harper studied at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Toronto" title="University of Toronto">University of Toronto's</a> <a href="/wiki/Trinity_College,_Toronto" title="Trinity College, Toronto">Trinity College</a> before moving to Alberta.<sup id="cite_ref-Johnson_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Johnson-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In an attempt to establish independence from his parents, Harper dropped out of the University of Toronto and then moved to <a href="/wiki/Edmonton" title="Edmonton">Edmonton</a>, Alberta, where he found work in the mail room at Imperial Oil.<sup id="cite_ref-Johnson_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Johnson-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Later, he advanced to work on the company's computer systems. He took up post-secondary studies again at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Calgary" title="University of Calgary">University of Calgary</a>, where he completed a bachelor's degree in economics in 1985. He later returned there to earn a master's degree in economics, completed in 1991.<sup id="cite_ref-Historica_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Historica-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Throughout his career, Harper has kept strong links to the University of Calgary. Trained as an economist, Harper was the first prime minister with an economics degree since <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a> and the first prime minister without a law degree since <a href="/wiki/Joe_Clark" title="Joe Clark">Joe Clark</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_beginnings">Political beginnings</span></h2>
<p>Harper became involved in politics as a member of his high school's <a href="/wiki/Young_Liberals_of_Canada" title="Young Liberals of Canada">Young Liberals club</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-canadiancontent1_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-canadiancontent1-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> He later changed his political allegiance because he disagreed with the <a href="/wiki/National_Energy_Program" title="National Energy Program">National Energy Program</a> (NEP) of Pierre Trudeau's Liberal government.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> He became executive assistant to Progressive Conservative (PC) Member of Parliament (MP) <a href="/wiki/Jim_Hawkes" title="Jim Hawkes">Jim Hawkes</a> in 1985 but later became disillusioned with the party and the government of <a href="/wiki/Brian_Mulroney" title="Brian Mulroney">Brian Mulroney</a>, citing the administration's economic policy.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> He left the PC Party the next year.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper was then recommended by the University of Calgary's economist Bob Mansell to <a href="/wiki/Preston_Manning" title="Preston Manning">Preston Manning</a>, the founder and leader of the <a href="/wiki/Right-wing_politics" title="Right-wing politics">right-wing</a> <a href="/wiki/Right-wing_populism" title="Right-wing populism">populist</a> <a href="/wiki/Reform_Party_of_Canada" title="Reform Party of Canada">Reform Party of Canada</a>. At that time Harper "didn't see himself as a politician", Mansell told CBC News in 2002, adding, "Politics was not his first love."<sup id="cite_ref-cbc-bio_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc-bio-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Manning invited him to participate in the party, and Harper gave a speech at Reform's 1987 founding convention in <a href="/wiki/Winnipeg" title="Winnipeg">Winnipeg</a>. He became the Reform Party's chief policy officer, and he played a major role in drafting the 1988 election platform,<sup id="cite_ref-cbc-bio_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc-bio-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> otherwise known as the Blue Book, which helped form the principles and policies of the party. Harper was influenced by his political mentor, <a href="/wiki/Tom_Flanagan_(political_scientist)" title="Tom Flanagan (political scientist)">Tom Flanagan</a>, when writing the book.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Harper is credited with creating Reform's campaign slogan, "The West wants in!"<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper ran for the House of Commons in the <a href="/wiki/1988_Canadian_federal_election" title="1988 Canadian federal election">1988 federal election</a> in Calgary West and losing by a wide margin to Hawkes, his former employer. After Reform candidate <a href="/wiki/Deborah_Grey" title="Deborah Grey">Deborah Grey</a> was elected as the party's first MP in a 1989 <a href="/wiki/By-election" title="By-election">by-election</a>, Harper became Grey's executive assistant, serving as her chief adviser and speechwriter until 1993.<sup id="cite_ref-Geoff_White_1989_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Geoff_White_1989-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He remained prominent in the Reform Party's national organization in his role as policy chief, encouraging the party to expand beyond its Western base and arguing that strictly regional parties were at risk of being taken over by radical elements.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> He delivered a speech at the Reform Party's 1991 national convention, in which he condemned extremist views.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper's relationship with Manning became strained in 1992, because of conflicting strategies over the <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown_Accord" title="Charlottetown Accord">Charlottetown Accord</a>. Harper opposed the accord on principle for ideological reasons, while Manning was initially more open to compromise. Harper also criticized Manning's decision to hire <a href="/wiki/Rick_Anderson_(political_strategist)" title="Rick Anderson (political strategist)">Rick Anderson</a> as an adviser, believing that Anderson was not sufficiently committed to the Reform Party's principles.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Harper resigned as the policy chief in October 1992.
</p><p>Harper stood for office again in the <a href="/wiki/1993_Canadian_federal_election" title="1993 Canadian federal election">1993 federal election</a> and defeated Jim Hawkes amid a significant Reform breakthrough in Western Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/National_Citizens_Coalition" title="National Citizens Coalition">National Citizens Coalition</a> (NCC) ran a $50,000 print and television campaign against Hawkes but did not endorse Harper directly.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Reform_MP_.281993.E2.80.931997.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Reform_MP_(1993–1997)">Reform MP (1993–1997)</span></h2>
<p>Harper emerged a prominent member of the Reform Party caucus. He was active on constitutional issues and played a prominent role in drafting the Reform Party's strategy for the <a href="/wiki/1995_Quebec_referendum" title="1995 Quebec referendum">1995 Quebec referendum</a>. A long-standing opponent of centralized <a href="/wiki/Canadian_federalism" title="Canadian federalism">federalism</a>, he stood with Preston Manning in Montreal to introduce a twenty-point plan to "decentralize and modernize" Canada in the event of a "no" victory.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Harper later argued that the "no" side's narrow <a href="/wiki/Plurality_(voting)" class="mw-redirect" title="Plurality (voting)">plurality</a> was a worst-case scenario, in that no-one had won a mandate for change.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper has expressed some <a href="/wiki/Social_conservatism" title="Social conservatism">socially conservative</a> views on certain issues.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> In 1994, he opposed plans by federal Justice Minister <a href="/wiki/Allan_Rock" title="Allan Rock">Allan Rock</a> to introduce spousal benefits for same-sex couples. Citing the recent failure of a similar initiative in Ontario, he was quoted as saying, "What I hope they learn is not to get into it. There are more important social and economic issues, not to mention the unity question."<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Harper also spoke against the possibility of the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Human_Rights_Commission" title="Canadian Human Rights Commission">Canadian Human Rights Commission</a> or the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court</a> changing federal policy in these and other matters.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the Reform Party's 1994 policy convention, Harper was part of a small minority of delegates who voted against restricting the definition of marriage to "the union of one man and one woman".<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> He opposed both <a href="/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Canada" title="Same-sex marriage in Canada">same-sex marriage</a> and mandated benefits for same-sex couples, but argued that political parties should refrain from taking official positions on these and other "issues of conscience".<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper was the only Reform MP to support the creation of the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Firearms_Registry" title="Canadian Firearms Registry">Canadian Firearms Registry</a> at <a href="/wiki/Second_reading" class="mw-redirect" title="Second reading">second reading</a> in 1995, although he later voted against it at <a href="/wiki/Third_reading" class="mw-redirect" title="Third reading">third reading</a> stage. He said at the time that he initially voted for the registry because of a poll showing that most of his constituents supported it, and added that he changed his vote when a second poll showed the opposite result. It was reported in April 1995, that some Progressive Conservatives opposed to <a href="/wiki/Jean_Charest" title="Jean Charest">Jean Charest</a>'s leadership wanted to remove both Charest and Manning, and unite the Reform and Progressive Conservative parties under Harper's leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite his prominent position in the party, Harper's relationship with the Reform Party leadership was frequently strained. In early 1994, he criticized a party decision to establish a personal expense account for Manning at a time when other Reform MPs had been asked to forego parliamentary perquisites.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> He was formally rebuked by the Reform executive council despite winning support from some MPs. His relationship with Manning grew increasingly fractious in the mid-1990s, and he pointedly declined to express any opinion on Manning's leadership during a 1996 interview.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> This friction was indicative of a fundamental divide between the two men: Harper was strongly committed to conservative principles and opposed Manning's inclinations toward <a href="/wiki/Populism" title="Populism">populism</a>, which Harper saw as leading to compromise on core ideological matters.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>These tensions culminated in late 1996 when Harper announced that he would not be a candidate in the <a href="/wiki/1997_Canadian_federal_election" title="1997 Canadian federal election">next federal election</a>. He resigned his parliamentary seat on January 14, 1997, the same day that he was appointed as a vice-president of the National Citizens Coalition (NCC), a conservative think-tank and advocacy group.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> He was promoted to NCC president later in the year.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 1997, Harper suggested that the Reform Party was drifting toward social conservatism and ignoring the principles of <a href="/wiki/Fiscal_conservatism" title="Fiscal conservatism">economic conservatism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> The Liberal Party lost seats but managed to retain a narrow majority government in the 1997 federal election, while Reform made only modest gains.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Out_of_parliament">Out of parliament</span></h2>
<h3><span id="1997.E2.80.932000"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1997–2000">1997–2000</span></h3>
<p>Soon after leaving Parliament, Harper and Tom Flanagan co-authored an opinion piece entitled "Our <a href="/wiki/Enlightened_absolutism" title="Enlightened absolutism">Benign Dictatorship</a>", which argued that the Liberal Party only retained power through a dysfunctional political system and a divided opposition. Harper and Flanagan argued that <a href="/wiki/National_conservative" class="mw-redirect" title="National conservative">national conservative</a> governments between 1917 and 1993 were founded on temporary alliances between Western populists and <a href="/wiki/Quebec_nationalism" title="Quebec nationalism">Quebec nationalists</a>, and were unable to govern because of their fundamental contradictions. The authors called for an alliance of Canada's conservative parties, and suggested that meaningful political change might require electoral reforms such as <a href="/wiki/Proportional_representation" title="Proportional representation">proportional representation</a>. "Our Benign Dictatorship" also commended <a href="/wiki/Conrad_Black" title="Conrad Black">Conrad Black</a>'s purchase of the <a href="/wiki/Southam_Newspapers" class="mw-redirect" title="Southam Newspapers">Southam</a> newspaper chain, arguing that his stewardship would provide for a "pluralistic" editorial view to counter the "monolithically liberal and <a href="/wiki/Feminism" title="Feminism">feminist</a>" approach of the previous management.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper remained active in constitutional issues. He was a prominent opponent of the <a href="/wiki/Calgary_Declaration" title="Calgary Declaration">Calgary Declaration</a> on national unity in late 1997, describing it as an "appeasement strategy" against Quebec nationalism. He called for federalist politicians to reject this strategy, and approach future constitutional talks from the position that "Quebec separatists are the problem and they need to be fixed".<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In late 1999, Harper called for the federal government to establish clear rules for any future Quebec referendum on sovereignty.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Some have identified Harper's views as an influence on the Chrétien government's <i><a href="/wiki/Clarity_Act" title="Clarity Act">Clarity Act</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As president of the National Citizens Coalition (NCC) from 1998 to 2002, Harper launched an ultimately unsuccessful <a href="/wiki/Harper_v._Canada_(Attorney_General)" class="mw-redirect" title="Harper v. Canada (Attorney General)">legal battle</a> against federal election laws restricting third-party advertising.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> He led the NCC in several campaigns against the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Wheat_Board" title="Canadian Wheat Board">Canadian Wheat Board</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> and supported Finance Minister <a href="/wiki/Paul_Martin" title="Paul Martin">Paul Martin</a>'s 2000 tax cuts as a positive first step toward tax reform.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1997, Harper delivered a controversial speech on <a href="/wiki/Canadian_identity" title="Canadian identity">Canadian identity</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Council_for_National_Policy" title="Council for National Policy">Council for National Policy</a>, a conservative American <a href="/wiki/Think_tank" title="Think tank">think tank</a>. He made comments such as "Canada is a Northern European <a href="/wiki/Welfare_state" title="Welfare state">welfare state</a> in the worst sense of the term, and very proud of it", "if you're like all Americans, you know almost nothing except for your own country. Which makes you probably knowledgeable about one more country than most Canadians", and "the <a href="/wiki/New_Democratic_Party_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="New Democratic Party (Canada)">NDP</a> [New Democratic Party] is kind of proof that the Devil lives and interferes in the affairs of men."<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> These statements were made public and criticized during the <a href="/wiki/2006_Canadian_federal_election" title="2006 Canadian federal election">2006 election</a>. Harper argued that the speech was intended as humour, and not as serious analysis.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper considered campaigning for the Progressive Conservative Party leadership in 1998, after Jean Charest left federal politics. Among those encouraging his candidacy were senior aides to Ontario <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Ontario" title="Premier of Ontario">Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/Mike_Harris" title="Mike Harris">Mike Harris</a>, including <a href="/wiki/Tony_Clement" title="Tony Clement">Tony Clement</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tom_Long_(politician)" title="Tom Long (politician)">Tom Long</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> He eventually decided against running, arguing that it would "burn bridges to those Reformers with whom I worked for many years" and prevent an alliance of right-wing parties from taking shape.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Harper was sceptical about the Reform Party's <a href="/wiki/United_Alternative" class="mw-redirect" title="United Alternative">United Alternative</a> initiative in 1999, arguing that it would serve to consolidate Manning's hold on the party leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> He also expressed concern that the UA would dilute Reform's ideological focus.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="2000.E2.80.932001"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="2000–2001">2000–2001</span></h3>
<p>When the United Alternative created the Canadian Alliance in 2000 as a successor party to Reform, Harper predicted that <a href="/wiki/Stockwell_Day" title="Stockwell Day">Stockwell Day</a> would defeat Preston Manning for the new party's leadership. He expressed reservations about Day's abilities, however, and accused Day of "[making] adherence to his social views a <a href="/wiki/Litmus_test_(politics)" title="Litmus test (politics)">litmus test</a> to determine whether you're in the party or not".<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Harper endorsed Tom Long for the leadership, arguing that Long was best suited to take support from the Progressive Conservative Party.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> When Day placed first on the first ballot, Harper said that the Canadian Alliance was shifting "more towards being a party of the <a href="/wiki/Christian_right" title="Christian right">religious right</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the death of Pierre Trudeau in 2000, Harper wrote an editorial criticizing Trudeau's policies as they affected Western Canada. He wrote that Trudeau "embraced the fashionable causes of his time, with variable enthusiasm and differing results", but "took a pass" on the issues that "truly defined his century".<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Harper subsequently accused Trudeau of promoting "unabashed socialism", and argued that Canadian governments between 1972 and 2002 had restricted <a href="/wiki/Economic_growth" title="Economic growth">economic growth</a> through "state corporatism".<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the Canadian Alliance's poor showing in the <a href="/wiki/2000_Canadian_federal_election" title="2000 Canadian federal election">2000 election</a>, Harper joined with other Western conservatives in co-authoring a document called the "<a href="/wiki/Alberta_Agenda" title="Alberta Agenda">Alberta Agenda</a>". The letter called on Alberta to reform publicly funded health care, replace the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Pension_Plan" title="Canada Pension Plan">Canada Pension Plan</a> with a provincial plan and replace the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Mounted_Police" title="Royal Canadian Mounted Police">Royal Canadian Mounted Police</a> with a provincial police force. It became known as the "firewall letter", because it called on the provincial government to "build firewalls around Alberta" to stop the federal government from redistributing its wealth to less affluent regions.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Alberta <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Alberta" title="Premier of Alberta">Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Klein" title="Ralph Klein">Ralph Klein</a> agreed with some of the letter's recommendations, but distanced himself from the "firewall" comments.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper also wrote an editorial in late 2000 arguing that Alberta and the rest of Canada were "embark[ing] on divergent and potentially hostile paths to defining their country". He said that Alberta had chosen the "best of Canada's heritage—a combination of American enterprise and individualism with the British traditions of order and co-operation" while Canada "appears content to become a second-tier socialistic country&#160;... led by a second-world strongman appropriately suited for the task". He also called for a "stronger and much more autonomous Alberta", while rejecting calls for <a href="/wiki/Alberta_separatism" title="Alberta separatism">separatism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/2001_Alberta_general_election" title="2001 Alberta general election">2001 Alberta provincial election</a>, Harper led the NCC in a "Vote Anything but Liberal" campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Some articles from this period described him as a possible successor to Klein.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper and the NCC endorsed a private school <a href="/wiki/Tax_credit" title="Tax credit">tax credit</a> proposed by Ontario's <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Ontario" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Ontario">Progressive Conservative</a> government in 2001, arguing that it would "save about $7,000 for each student who does not attend a <a href="/wiki/Trade_union" title="Trade union">union</a>-run public school". <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Education_(Ontario)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Education (Ontario)">Education Minister</a> <a href="/wiki/Janet_Ecker" title="Janet Ecker">Janet Ecker</a> criticized this, saying that her government's intent was not to save money at the expense of public education.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Day's leadership of the Canadian Alliance became increasingly troubled throughout the summer of 2001, as several party MPs called for his resignation. In June, the <i><a href="/wiki/National_Post" title="National Post">National Post</a></i> newspaper reported that former Reform MP <a href="/wiki/Ian_McClelland" title="Ian McClelland">Ian McClelland</a> was organizing a possible leadership challenge on Harper's behalf.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Harper announced his resignation from the NCC presidency in August 2001, to prepare a campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Canadian_Alliance_leadership.2C_2002.E2.80.932003"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Canadian_Alliance_leadership,_2002–2003">Canadian Alliance leadership, 2002–2003</span></h2>
<p>Stockwell Day called a new Canadian Alliance leadership race for 2002, and soon declared himself a candidate. Harper emerged as Day's main rival, and declared his own candidacy on December 3, 2001. He eventually won the support of at least 28 Alliance MPs,<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> including <a href="/wiki/Scott_Reid_(politician)" title="Scott Reid (politician)">Scott Reid</a>, <a href="/wiki/James_Rajotte" title="James Rajotte">James Rajotte</a><sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Keith_Martin_(politician)" title="Keith Martin (politician)">Keith Martin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> During the campaign, Harper reprised his earlier warnings against an alliance with Quebec nationalists, and called for his party to become the federalist option in Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> He argued that "the French language is not imperilled in Quebec", and opposed "special status" for the province in the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Constitution">Canadian constitution</a> accordingly.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> He also endorsed greater provincial autonomy on <a href="/wiki/Medicare_(Canada)" title="Medicare (Canada)">Medicare</a>, and said that he would not co-operate with the Progressive Conservatives as long as they were led by Joe Clark.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> On social issues, Harper argued for "parental rights" to use <a href="/wiki/Corporal_punishment" title="Corporal punishment">corporal punishment</a> against their children and supported raising the age of sexual consent.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> He described his potential support base as "similar to what <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George Bush</a> tapped".<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The tone of the leadership contest turned hostile in February 2002. Harper described Day's governance of the party as "amateurish",<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> while his campaign team argued that Day was attempting to win re-election by building a narrow support base among different groups in the religious right.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> The Day campaign accused Harper of "attacking ethnic and religious minorities".<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> In early March, the two candidates had an especially fractious debate on <a href="/wiki/CBC_Newsworld" class="mw-redirect" title="CBC Newsworld">CBC Newsworld</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> The leadership vote was held on March 20, 2002. Harper was elected on the first ballot with 55% support, against 37% for Day. Two other candidates split the remainder.
</p><p>After winning the party leadership, Harper announced his intention to run for parliament in a by-election in Calgary Southwest, recently vacated by Preston Manning. <a href="/wiki/Ezra_Levant" title="Ezra Levant">Ezra Levant</a> had been chosen as the riding's Alliance candidate and declared that he would not stand aside for Harper; he later reconsidered.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> The Liberals did not field a candidate, following a parliamentary tradition of allowing opposition leaders to enter the House of Commons unopposed. The Progressive Conservative candidate, <a href="/wiki/Jim_Prentice" title="Jim Prentice">Jim Prentice</a>, also chose to withdraw.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Harper was elected without difficulty over |New Democrat <a href="/wiki/Bill_Phipps" title="Bill Phipps">Bill Phipps</a>, a former <a href="/wiki/United_Church_of_Canada" title="United Church of Canada">United Church of Canada</a> moderator. Harper told a reporter during the campaign that he "despise[d]" Phipps, and declined to debate him.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper officially became the <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">leader of the Official Opposition</a> in May 2002. Later in the same month, he said that the <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_Provinces" class="mw-redirect" title="Atlantic Provinces">Atlantic Provinces</a> were trapped in "a culture of defeat" which had to be overcome, the result of policies designed by Liberal and Progressive Conservative governments. Many Atlantic politicians condemned the remark as patronizing and insensitive. The <a href="/wiki/Legislature_of_Nova_Scotia" class="mw-redirect" title="Legislature of Nova Scotia">Legislature of Nova Scotia</a> unanimously approved a motion condemning Harper's comments,<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> which were also criticized by <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_New_Brunswick" title="Premier of New Brunswick">New Brunswick premier</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bernard_Lord" title="Bernard Lord">Bernard Lord</a>, federal Progressive Conservative leader Joe Clark and others. Harper refused to apologize, and said that much of Canada was trapped by the same "can't-do" attitude.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 2003, their speeches in favour gaining no traction in Parliament, Harper and Stockwell Day co-wrote a letter to <i><a href="/wiki/The_Wall_Street_Journal" title="The Wall Street Journal">The Wall Street Journal</a></i> in which they condemned the Canadian government's unwillingness to participate in the <a href="/wiki/2003_invasion_of_Iraq" title="2003 invasion of Iraq">2003 invasion of Iraq</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As party leader, Harper sought to merge the Alliance with the Progressive Conservatives (PCs) to create a united <a href="/wiki/Right-of-centre" class="mw-redirect" title="Right-of-centre">right-of-centre</a> party. The possibility of a united conservative party increased after <a href="/wiki/Peter_Mackay" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter Mackay">Peter Mackay</a> was <a href="/wiki/2003_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_election" title="2003 Progressive Conservative leadership election">elected</a> Progressive Conservative leader in May 2003. On October 16, 2003, Harper and Mackay agreed to merge the two parties to form the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Conservative Party of Canada">Conservative Party of Canada</a>. After 95 percent of Alliance members voted in favour of merging with the PCs and 90 percent of 2,486 PC delegates voted in favour of merging with the Alliance, the Conservative Party of Canada was founded on December 7, 2003.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Conservative_Party_leadership.2C_2004.E2.80.932006"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Conservative_Party_leadership,_2004–2006">Conservative Party leadership, 2004–2006</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/2004_Conservative_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="2004 Conservative Party of Canada leadership election">2004 Conservative Party of Canada leadership election</a></div>
<p>On January 12, 2004, Harper announced his resignation as the leader of the Official Opposition in order to run for the leadership of the Conservative Party of Canada. Harper was elected the first leader of the Conservative Party, with a first ballot majority against <a href="/wiki/Belinda_Stronach" title="Belinda Stronach">Belinda Stronach</a> and Tony Clement on March 20, 2004. Harper's victory included strong showings outside of Western Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-cpac092613_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cpac092613-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-2004ridings_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2004ridings-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2004_federal_election">2004 federal election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2004_Canadian_federal_election" title="2004 Canadian federal election">2004 Canadian federal election</a></div>
<p>Harper led the Conservatives into the 2004 federal election. Initially, new Prime Minister Paul Martin held a large lead in polls, but this eroded because of infighting, <a href="/wiki/Sponsorship_scandal" title="Sponsorship scandal">Adscam</a> (a <a href="/wiki/Scandal" title="Scandal">scandal</a> that came as a result of a <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Canada" title="Government of Canada">Government of Canada</a> "<a href="/wiki/Sponsor_(commercial)" title="Sponsor (commercial)">sponsorship</a> program" in the province of Quebec and involving the Liberal Party of Canada) and other scandals surrounding his government. The Liberals attempted to counter this with an early election call, as this would give the Conservatives less time to consolidate their merger.<sup id="cite_ref-nasty2004_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nasty2004-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This, along with an unpopular provincial budget by Liberal Premier <a href="/wiki/Dalton_McGuinty" title="Dalton McGuinty">Dalton McGuinty</a> in Ontario, moved the Conservatives into a lead for a time.<sup id="cite_ref-nasty2004_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nasty2004-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> However, comments by Conservative MPs, leaked press releases accusing the then prime minister of supporting <a href="/wiki/Child_pornography" title="Child pornography">child pornography</a>, as well as <a href="/wiki/Attack_ad" title="Attack ad">attack ads</a> suggesting that the Conservatives had a secret agenda, caused Harper's party to lose some momentum.<sup id="cite_ref-np042111_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-np042111-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-cbc062004_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc062004-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-po030106_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-po030106-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Liberals were re-elected to power with a minority government, with the Conservatives coming in second place. The Conservatives managed to make inroads into the Liberals' Ontario stronghold, primarily in the province's socially conservative central region. However, they were shut out of Quebec, marking the first time that a centre-right party did not win any seats in that province. Harper, after some personal deliberation, decided to stay on as the party leader.<sup id="cite_ref-cbc120319_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc120319-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Agreement_with_the_BQ_and_the_NDP">Agreement with the BQ and the NDP</span></h4>
<p>Two months after the federal election, Harper privately met <a href="/wiki/Bloc_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois" title="Bloc Québécois">Bloc Québécois</a> leader <a href="/wiki/Gilles_Duceppe" title="Gilles Duceppe">Gilles Duceppe</a> and New Democratic Party leader <a href="/wiki/Jack_Layton" title="Jack Layton">Jack Layton</a> in a Montreal hotel.<sup id="cite_ref-gazetteCo-Opposition_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gazetteCo-Opposition-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> On September 9, 2004, the three signed a letter addressed to the <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">governor general</a>, <a href="/wiki/Adrienne_Clarkson" title="Adrienne Clarkson">Adrienne Clarkson</a>, stating, "We respectfully point out that the opposition parties, who together constitute a majority in the House, have been in close consultation. We believe that, should a request for dissolution arise this should give you cause, as constitutional practice has determined, to consult the opposition leaders and consider all of your options before exercising your constitutional authority."<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> On the same day the letter was written, the three party leaders held a joint press conference at which they expressed their intent to co-operate on changing parliamentary rules, and to request that the governor general consult with them before deciding to call an election.<sup id="cite_ref-Harpercoalition_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harpercoalition-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> At the news conference, Harper said, "It is the Parliament that's supposed to run the country, not just the largest party and the single leader of that party. That's a criticism I've had and that we've had and that most Canadians have had for a long, long time now so this is an opportunity to start to change that." At the time, Harper and the two other opposition leaders denied trying to form a <a href="/wiki/Coalition_government" title="Coalition government">coalition government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-gazetteCo-Opposition_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gazetteCo-Opposition-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Harper said, "This is not a coalition, but this is a co-operative effort."<sup id="cite_ref-Harpercoalition_102-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harpercoalition-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On October 4, <a href="/wiki/Mike_Duffy" title="Mike Duffy">Mike Duffy</a>, who was later appointed as a Conservative senator by Harper, said: "It is possible that you could change prime minister without having an election." He added that some Conservatives wanted Harper to temporarily become prime minister without holding an election. The next day, Layton walked out on talks with Harper and Duceppe, accusing them of trying to replace Paul Martin with Harper as prime minister. Both Bloc and Conservative officials denied Layton's accusations.<sup id="cite_ref-gazetteCo-Opposition_99-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gazetteCo-Opposition-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> On March 26, 2011, Duceppe stated that Harper had tried to form a coalition government with the Bloc and NDP in response to Harper's allegations that the Liberals may form a coalition with the Bloc and the NDP.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition">Leader of the Opposition</span></h3>
<p>The Conservative Party's first policy convention was held from March 17 to 19, 2005, in <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>. Harper had been rumoured to be shifting his ideology closer to that of a <a href="/wiki/Blue_Tory" title="Blue Tory">Blue Tory</a>, and many thought he'd wanted to move the party's policies closer to the centre. Any opposition to abortion or bilingualism was dropped from the Conservative platform. Harper received an 84% endorsement from delegates in the <a href="/wiki/Leadership_review" title="Leadership review">leadership review</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite the party abandoning debate over the two controversial issues, they began a concerted drive against same-sex marriage. Harper was criticized by a group of law professors for arguing that the government could override the provincial court rulings on same-sex marriage without using the "<a href="/wiki/Section_Thirty-three_of_the_Canadian_Charter_of_Rights_and_Freedoms" class="mw-redirect" title="Section Thirty-three of the Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms">notwithstanding clause</a>", a provision of the <i><a href="/wiki/Canadian_Charter_of_Rights_and_Freedoms" title="Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms">Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-gm011905_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gm011905-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> He also argued, in general, for lower taxes, an elected <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senate</a>, a tougher stance on crime, and closer relations with the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the April 2005 release of <a href="/wiki/Jean_Brault" class="mw-redirect" title="Jean Brault">Jean Brault</a>'s damaging testimony at the <a href="/wiki/Gomery_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="Gomery Commission">Gomery Commission</a>, implicating the Liberals in the scandal, opinion polls placed the Conservatives ahead of Liberals.<sup id="cite_ref-sink42505_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sink42505-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> The Conservatives had earlier abstained from the vote on the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_federal_budget,_2005" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian federal budget, 2005">2005 budget</a> to avoid forcing an election.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> With the collapse in Liberal support and a controversial NDP amendment to the budget, the party exerted significant pressure on Harper to bring down the government.<sup id="cite_ref-cbc062405_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc062405-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> In May, Harper announced that Martin's Liberals had lost the "moral authority to govern".<sup id="cite_ref-nyt051105_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nyt051105-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Shortly thereafter, the Conservatives and Bloc Québécois united to defeat the government on a vote that some considered to be either a <a href="/wiki/Confidence_motion" class="mw-redirect" title="Confidence motion">confidence motion</a> or else a motion requiring an immediate test of the confidence of the House.<sup id="cite_ref-dam53005_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dam53005-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> The Martin government did not accept this interpretation and argued that vote had been on a procedural motion, although they also indicated that they would bring forward their revised budget for a confidence vote the following week. Ultimately, the effort to bring down the Martin government failed following the decision of Conservative MP Belinda Stronach to <a href="/wiki/Crossing_the_floor" title="Crossing the floor">cross the floor</a> to the Liberal Party. The vote on the NDP amendment to the budget tied, and with the <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_House_of_Commons_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Speaker of the House of Commons of Canada">speaker of the House</a> voting to continue the debate, the Liberals stayed in power. At the time, some considered the matter to be a <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_crisis" title="Constitutional crisis">constitutional crisis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-dam53005_112-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dam53005-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Heard,_Andrew,_The_Confidence_Convention_and_the_May_2005_Vote_on_the_Public_Accounts_Committee_Report_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Heard,_Andrew,_The_Confidence_Convention_and_the_May_2005_Vote_on_the_Public_Accounts_Committee_Report-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Bliss,_Michael_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bliss,_Michael-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper was also criticized for supporting his caucus colleague MP <a href="/wiki/Gurmant_Grewal" title="Gurmant Grewal">Gurmant Grewal</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Grewal had produced tapes of conversations with <a href="/wiki/Tim_Murphy_(Canadian_politician)" title="Tim Murphy (Canadian politician)">Tim Murphy</a>, Paul Martin's chief of staff, in which Grewal claimed he had been offered a cabinet position in exchange for his defection.<sup id="cite_ref-cbc053105_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc053105-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Liberals' support dropped sharply after the first report from the Gomery Commission was issued, but rebounded soon after.<sup id="cite_ref-flawless6_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-flawless6-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Later that month, Harper introduced a motion of no confidence on the Martin government, telling the House of Commons "that this government has lost the confidence of the House of Commons and needs to be removed". As the Liberals had lost NDP support in the house by refusing to accept an NDP plan to prevent health care privatization, the no-confidence motion was passed by a vote of 171–133. It was the first time that a Canadian government had been toppled by a straight motion of no confidence proposed by the opposition. As a result, Parliament was dissolved and a general election was scheduled for January 23, 2006.<sup id="cite_ref-flawless6_117-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-flawless6-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-jack1255_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jack1255-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-cbc112805_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc112805-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On February 27, 2008, allegations surfaced that two Conservative Party officials offered terminally ill, independent MP <a href="/wiki/Chuck_Cadman#2005_Budget_vote" title="Chuck Cadman">Chuck Cadman</a> a million-dollar life insurance policy in exchange for his vote to bring down the Liberal government in a May 2005, budget vote.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> If the story had been proved true, the actions may have been grounds for charges as a criminal offence as under the <i><a href="/wiki/Criminal_Code_(Canada)" title="Criminal Code (Canada)">Criminal Code</a></i>, it is illegal to bribe an MP.<sup id="cite_ref-cbc-2008-02-27_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc-2008-02-27-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When asked by Vancouver journalist Tom Zytaruk about the alleged life insurance offer then-opposition leader Stephen Harper states on an audio tape "I don't know the details. I know there were discussions"<sup id="cite_ref-investigate1_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-investigate1-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> and goes on to say "The offer to Chuck was that it was only to replace financial considerations he might lose due to an election".<sup id="cite_ref-investigate1_122-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-investigate1-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Harper also stated that he had told the Conservative Party representatives that they were unlikely to succeed. "I told them they were wasting their time. I said Chuck had made up his mind."<sup id="cite_ref-investigate1_122-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-investigate1-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-articlenews1_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-articlenews1-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> In February 2008, the Royal Canadian Mounted Police (RCMP) investigated the allegations that Section 119's provisions on bribery and corruption in the <i>Criminal Code</i> had been violated.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> The RCMP concluded their investigation stating that there was no evidence for pressing charges.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper denied any wrongdoing and subsequently filed a civil <a href="/wiki/Libel" class="mw-redirect" title="Libel">libel</a> suit against the Liberal Party. Because libel laws do not apply to statements made in Parliament, the basis of the lawsuit was that statements made by Liberal Party members outside the House of Commons and in articles which appeared on the Liberal Party web site made accusations that Harper had committed a criminal act.<sup id="cite_ref-articlenews1_123-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-articlenews1-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The audio expert hired by Harper to prove that the tape containing the evidence was doctored reported that the latter part of the tape was recorded over, but the tape was unaltered where Harper's voice said "I don't know the details, I know that, um, there were discussions, um, but this is not for publication?" and goes on to say he "didn't know the details" when asked if he knew anything about the alleged offer to Cadman.<sup id="cite_ref-cbctape_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbctape-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2006_federal_election">2006 federal election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2006_Canadian_federal_election" title="2006 Canadian federal election">2006 Canadian federal election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Harper,-Stephen-Jan-23-06.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Harper%2C-Stephen-Jan-23-06.jpg/220px-Harper%2C-Stephen-Jan-23-06.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Harper%2C-Stephen-Jan-23-06.jpg/330px-Harper%2C-Stephen-Jan-23-06.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Harper%2C-Stephen-Jan-23-06.jpg/440px-Harper%2C-Stephen-Jan-23-06.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2132" data-file-height="2132" /></a><figcaption>Stephen Harper giving a victory speech to party members in <a href="/wiki/Calgary" title="Calgary">Calgary</a> after the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Conservative Party of Canada">Conservatives</a> won the <a href="/wiki/2006_Canadian_federal_election" title="2006 Canadian federal election">2006 federal election</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Conservatives began the campaign period with a policy-per-day strategy, contrary to the Liberal plan of holding off major announcements until after the Christmas holidays, so Harper dominated media coverage for the first weeks of the election. Though his party showed only modest movement in the polls, Harper's personal numbers, which had always significantly trailed those of his party, began to rise. In response, the Liberals launched <a href="/wiki/2006_Harper_attack_ads" class="mw-redirect" title="2006 Harper attack ads">negative ads targeting Harper</a>, similar to their attacks in the 2004 election. However, their tactics were not sufficient to erode the Conservative's advantage, although they did manage to close what had been a ten-point advantage in public opinion. As Harper's personal numbers rose, polls found he was now considered not only more trustworthy, but a better choice for prime minister than Martin.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Immediately prior to the Christmas break, in a faxed letter to NDP candidate <a href="/wiki/Judy_Wasylycia-Leis" title="Judy Wasylycia-Leis">Judy Wasylycia-Leis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Commissioner_of_the_RCMP" class="mw-redirect" title="Commissioner of the RCMP">RCMP commissioner</a> <a href="/wiki/Giuliano_Zaccardelli" title="Giuliano Zaccardelli">Giuliano Zaccardelli</a> announced the RCMP had opened a criminal investigation into her complaint that it appeared Liberal Finance Minister <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Goodale" title="Ralph Goodale">Ralph Goodale</a>'s office had leaked information leading to <a href="/wiki/Insider_trading" title="Insider trading">insider trading</a> before making an important announcement on the taxation of <a href="/wiki/Income_trust" title="Income trust">income trusts</a>. On December 27, 2005, the RCMP confirmed that information in a press release. At the conclusion of the investigation, Serge Nadeau, a top civil servant in the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Finance_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Department of Finance (Canada)">Department of Finance</a>, was charged with criminal breach of trust. No charges were laid against Goodale.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The election gave Harper's Conservatives the largest number of seats in the House, although not enough for a majority government, and shortly after midnight on January 24, Martin conceded defeat. Later that day, Martin informed Governor General <a href="/wiki/Micha%C3%ABlle_Jean" title="Michaëlle Jean">Michaëlle Jean</a> that he would resign as prime minister, and at 6:45&#160;p.m. Jean asked Harper to form a government. Harper was sworn in as Canada's 22nd prime minister on February 6, 2006.
</p><p>In his first address to Parliament as prime minister, Harper opened by paying tribute to the <a href="/wiki/Monarchy_of_Canada" title="Monarchy of Canada">queen of Canada</a>, <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Elizabeth II</a>, and her "lifelong dedication to duty and self-sacrifice".<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> He also said before the Canada-UK Chamber of Commerce that Canada and the United Kingdom were joined by "the golden circle of <a href="/wiki/The_Crown" title="The Crown">the Crown</a>, which links us all together with the majestic past that takes us back to the <a href="/wiki/Tudors" class="mw-redirect" title="Tudors">Tudors</a>, the Plantagenets, the <a href="/wiki/Magna_Carta" title="Magna Carta">Magna Carta</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Habeas_corpus" title="Habeas corpus">habeas corpus</a></i>, petition of rights, and English <a href="/wiki/Common_law" title="Common law">common law</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Journalist Graham Fraser said in the <i><a href="/wiki/Toronto_Star" title="Toronto Star">Toronto Star</a></i> that Harper's speech was "one of the most monarchist speeches a Canadian prime minister has given since <a href="/wiki/John_Diefenbaker" title="John Diefenbaker">John Diefenbaker</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> An analysis by <a href="/wiki/Michael_D._Behiels" title="Michael D. Behiels">Michael D. Behiels</a> suggested that a political realignment might be underway, based on the continuance of Harper's government.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the election, the Conservative party were charged with improper election spending, in a case that became known as the <a href="/wiki/In_and_Out_scandal" title="In and Out scandal">In and Out scandal</a>. It dragged on for years, but in 2012 they took a plea deal, admitting both improper spending and falsifying records to hide it.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_of_Canada_.282006.E2.80.932015.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_of_Canada_(2006–2015)">Prime Minister of Canada (2006–2015)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1045330069"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1220487116"><table class="sidebar sidebar-collapse nomobile nowraplinks"><tbody><tr><td class="sidebar-pretitle">This article is part of <a href="/wiki/Category:Conservatism_in_Canada" title="Category:Conservatism in Canada">a series</a> on</td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-title-with-pretitle" style="background:#1034A6; padding-top:0.25em; font-size:160%; font-weight:normal; color:white; line-height:1em"><a href="/wiki/Conservatism_in_Canada" title="Conservatism in Canada"><span class="tmp-color" style="color:#FFF">Conservatism in Canada</span></a></th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-image"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Royal_Coat_of_arms_of_Canada.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Royal_Coat_of_arms_of_Canada.svg/90px-Royal_Coat_of_arms_of_Canada.svg.png" decoding="async" width="90" height="124" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Royal_Coat_of_arms_of_Canada.svg/135px-Royal_Coat_of_arms_of_Canada.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Royal_Coat_of_arms_of_Canada.svg/180px-Royal_Coat_of_arms_of_Canada.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="706" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content">

<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:transparent; border-top:1px solid #AAA; text-align:center">Politicians</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content hlist">

<ul><li><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="flag" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Maple_Leaf_%28from_roundel%29.svg/14px-Maple_Leaf_%28from_roundel%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="14" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Maple_Leaf_%28from_roundel%29.svg/21px-Maple_Leaf_%28from_roundel%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Maple_Leaf_%28from_roundel%29.svg/28px-Maple_Leaf_%28from_roundel%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="305" data-file-height="343" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Canada" title="Portal:Canada">Canada&#32;portal</a></li>
<li><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/15px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png" decoding="async" width="15" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/22px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/30px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="249" data-file-height="268" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Conservatism" title="Portal:Conservatism">Conservatism&#32;portal</a></li></ul></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Conservatism_in_Canada" title="Template:Conservatism in Canada"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Conservatism_in_Canada" title="Template talk:Conservatism in Canada"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Conservatism_in_Canada" title="Special:EditPage/Template:Conservatism in Canada"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Premiership_of_Stephen_Harper" title="Premiership of Stephen Harper">Premiership of Stephen Harper</a>, <a href="/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_the_Stephen_Harper_government" title="Domestic policy of the Stephen Harper government">Domestic policy of the Stephen Harper government</a>, <a href="/wiki/Environmental_policy_of_the_Stephen_Harper_government" title="Environmental policy of the Stephen Harper government">Environmental policy of the Stephen Harper government</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Stephen_Harper_government" title="Foreign policy of the Stephen Harper government">Foreign policy of the Stephen Harper government</a></div>
<p>In July 2019, a group of independent academics published an assessment of past prime ministers of Canada based on the number of campaign pledges and promises fulfilled. According to the study, the Harper government fulfilled 85 per cent of its pledges (including partially-completed pledges). When factoring only completed, realized pledges, the Harper's government, in their last year, kept 77 per cent of promises. The study found that the governments led by Harper, in addition to the government led by his successor, <a href="/wiki/Justin_Trudeau" title="Justin Trudeau">Justin Trudeau</a>, had the highest rates of follow-through for campaign promises of any Canadian government in the last 35 years.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2008_federal_election">2008 federal election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2008_Canadian_federal_election" title="2008 Canadian federal election">2008 Canadian federal election</a></div>
<p>On October 14, 2008, after a 5-week-long campaign, the Conservatives increased their seat count in Parliament to 143, up from 127 at the dissolution of the previous Parliament; however, the actual popular vote among Canadians dropped slightly by 167,494 votes. As a result of the lowest <a href="/wiki/2008_Canadian_federal_election#Voter_turnout" title="2008 Canadian federal election">voter turnout</a> in Canadian electoral history, this represented only 22% of eligible Canadian voters, the lowest level of support of any winning party in Canadian history.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Meanwhile, the number of opposition Liberal MPs fell from 95 to 77 seats. 155 MPs are required to form a majority government in Canada's 308-seat parliament, relegating Harper to minority government once again.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2008_parliamentary_dispute_and_prorogation">2008 parliamentary dispute and prorogation</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2008%E2%80%932009_Canadian_parliamentary_dispute" title="2008–2009 Canadian parliamentary dispute">2008–2009 Canadian parliamentary dispute</a></div>
<p>On December 4, 2008, Harper asked Governor General Michaëlle Jean to <a href="/wiki/Prorogation_in_Canada" title="Prorogation in Canada">prorogue</a> Parliament to avoid a vote of confidence scheduled for the following Monday, becoming the first Canadian prime minister to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> The request was granted by Jean, and the prorogation lasted until January 26, 2009. The opposition coalition dissolved shortly after, with the Conservatives winning a Liberal supported confidence vote on January 29, 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2010_prorogation">2010 prorogation</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/2010_Canada_anti-prorogation_protests" title="2010 Canada anti-prorogation protests">2010 Canada anti-prorogation protests</a> and <a href="/wiki/Prorogation_in_Canada" title="Prorogation in Canada">Prorogation in Canada</a></div>
