<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Prime_Minister_of_Canada_Stephen_Harper_%26_Governor_General_of_Canada_Micha%C3%ABlle_Jean_@_2010_Vancouver_Olympic_Winter_Games_Heads_of_State_Reception.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Prime_Minister_of_Canada_Stephen_Harper_%26_Governor_General_of_Canada_Micha%C3%ABlle_Jean_%40_2010_Vancouver_Olympic_Winter_Games_Heads_of_State_Reception.jpg/220px-Prime_Minister_of_Canada_Stephen_Harper_%26_Governor_General_of_Canada_Micha%C3%ABlle_Jean_%40_2010_Vancouver_Olympic_Winter_Games_Heads_of_State_Reception.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="161" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Prime_Minister_of_Canada_Stephen_Harper_%26_Governor_General_of_Canada_Micha%C3%ABlle_Jean_%40_2010_Vancouver_Olympic_Winter_Games_Heads_of_State_Reception.jpg/330px-Prime_Minister_of_Canada_Stephen_Harper_%26_Governor_General_of_Canada_Micha%C3%ABlle_Jean_%40_2010_Vancouver_Olympic_Winter_Games_Heads_of_State_Reception.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Prime_Minister_of_Canada_Stephen_Harper_%26_Governor_General_of_Canada_Micha%C3%ABlle_Jean_%40_2010_Vancouver_Olympic_Winter_Games_Heads_of_State_Reception.jpg/440px-Prime_Minister_of_Canada_Stephen_Harper_%26_Governor_General_of_Canada_Micha%C3%ABlle_Jean_%40_2010_Vancouver_Olympic_Winter_Games_Heads_of_State_Reception.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1559" data-file-height="1142" /></a><figcaption>Prime Minister Stephen Harper &amp; <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General</a> <a href="/wiki/Micha%C3%ABlle_Jean" title="Michaëlle Jean">Michaëlle Jean</a> at <a href="/wiki/2010_Winter_Olympics" title="2010 Winter Olympics">2010 Vancouver Winter Olympic Games</a> Heads of State Reception.</figcaption></figure>
<p>On December 30, 2009, Harper announced that he would request the governor general to prorogue Parliament again, effective immediately on December 30, 2009, during the 2010 Winter Olympics and lasting until March 3, 2010. Harper stated that this was necessary for Canada's economic plan. Jean granted the request. In an interview with <a href="/wiki/CBC_News" title="CBC News">CBC News</a>, <a href="/wiki/Prince_Edward_Island" title="Prince Edward Island">Prince Edward Island</a> Liberal MP <a href="/wiki/Wayne_Easter" title="Wayne Easter">Wayne Easter</a> accused the prime minister of "shutting democracy down".<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Tom Flanagan, Harper's University of Calgary mentor and former chief of staff, also questioned Harper's reasoning for prorogation, stating that "I think the government's talking points haven't been entirely credible" and that the government's explanation of proroguing was "skirting the real issue—which is the harm the opposition parties are trying to do to the Canadian Forces" regarding the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Afghan_detainee_issue" title="Canadian Afghan detainee issue">Canadian Afghan detainee issue</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> Small demonstrations took place on January 23 in 64 Canadian cities and towns and five cities in other countries.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> A <a href="/wiki/Facebook" title="Facebook">Facebook</a> protest group attracted over 20,000 members.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A poll released by <a href="/wiki/Angus_Reid_Public_Opinion" class="mw-redirect" title="Angus Reid Public Opinion">Angus Reid</a> on January 7, found that 53 per cent of respondents were opposed to the prorogation, while 19 per cent supported it. 38 per cent believed Harper used the prorogation to curtail the Afghan detainee inquiry, while 23 per cent agreed with Harper's explanation that the prorogation was necessary economically.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2010_Senate_appointments">2010 Senate appointments</span></h3>
<p>Harper, on January 29, 2010, advised the governor general to appoint new Conservative senators to fill five vacancies in the Senate, one each for Quebec, Newfoundland and Labrador, and New Brunswick, and two for Ontario. The new senators were <a href="/wiki/Pierre-Hugues_Boisvenu" title="Pierre-Hugues Boisvenu">Pierre-Hugues Boisvenu</a>, of Quebec; <a href="/wiki/Bob_Runciman" title="Bob Runciman">Bob Runciman</a>, of Ontario; <a href="/wiki/Vim_Kochhar" title="Vim Kochhar">Vim Kochhar</a>, of Ontario; <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Marshall_(politician)" title="Elizabeth Marshall (politician)">Elizabeth Marshall</a> of <a href="/wiki/Newfoundland_and_Labrador" title="Newfoundland and Labrador">Newfoundland and Labrador</a>; and <a href="/wiki/Rose-May_Poirier" title="Rose-May Poirier">Rose-May Poirier</a>, of New Brunswick. This changed the party standings in the Senate, which had previously been dominated by Liberals, to 51 Conservatives, 49 Liberals, and five others.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2011_vote_of_no_confidence">2011 vote of no confidence</span></h3>
<p>Harper's Cabinet was defeated in a no-confidence vote on March 25, 2011, after being found in contempt of Parliament. Harper thus, in accordance with constitutional convention, advised the governor general to call a <a href="/wiki/2011_Canadian_federal_election" title="2011 Canadian federal election">general election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> This was the first occurrence in <a href="/wiki/Contempt_of_Parliament#Contempt_citation_cases_for_governments" class="mw-redirect" title="Contempt of Parliament">Commonwealth history</a> of a government in the Westminster parliamentary tradition losing the confidence of the <a href="/wiki/Lower_house" title="Lower house">lower house</a> on the grounds of contempt of parliament. The no-confidence motion was carried with a vote of 156 in favour of the motion and 145 against.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2011_election">2011 election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2011_Canadian_federal_election" title="2011 Canadian federal election">2011 Canadian federal election</a></div>
<p>On May 2, 2011, after a five-week campaign, Harper led the Conservatives to their third consecutive election victory—the first time a centre-right party has accomplished this in half a century. The Conservatives increased their standing in parliament to 166, up from 143 at the dissolution of the previous parliament. This resulted in the first centre-right majority government since the Progressive Conservatives had won their last majority in 1988. The Conservatives also received a greater number of total votes than in 2008. Notably, the Conservatives had a significant breakthrough in southern Ontario, a region where neither they nor the Reform/Alliance side of the merger had done well in the previous two decades. They managed to win several seats in Toronto itself; no centre-right party had won seats in the former <a href="/wiki/Metro_Toronto" class="mw-redirect" title="Metro Toronto">Metro Toronto</a> since 1988.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The election ended five years of minority governments, made the New Democratic Party the <a href="/wiki/Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Official Opposition (Canada)">Official Opposition</a> for the first time, relegated the Liberals to third place for the first time, brought <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_May" title="Elizabeth May">Elizabeth May</a> as Canada's first <a href="/wiki/Green_Party_of_Canada" title="Green Party of Canada">Green Party</a> Member of Parliament, and reduced the Bloc Québécois from 47 to 4 seats.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the election, the Conservatives were accused of cheating in the <a href="/wiki/Robocall_scandal" class="mw-redirect" title="Robocall scandal">Robocall scandal</a>, mainly suppressing votes by directing voters to bogus polling stations.<sup id="cite_ref-MaherFeb27_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MaherFeb27-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> There were complaints in 247 of Canada's 308 ridings, but only one person was charged; Conservative staffer <a href="/wiki/Michael_Sona" title="Michael Sona">Michael Sona</a> was convicted and jailed.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="2015_election">2015 election</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2015_Canadian_federal_election" title="2015 Canadian federal election">2015 Canadian federal election</a></div>
<p>Under the <i><a href="/wiki/Canada_Elections_Act" title="Canada Elections Act">Canada Elections Act</a></i>, a general election had to take place no later than October 19, 2015. On August 2, at Harper's request, Governor General David Johnson dropped the writs of election for October 19. In that election, Harper's Conservative Party was defeated by Justin Trudeau's Liberals, and became the Official Opposition, dropping to only 99 seats out of 338. This was mainly because of a collapse of Conservative support in southern Ontario, a region that swung heavily to them in 2011. They lost all of their seats in Toronto, and won only three seats in the Greater Toronto Area. They were also shut out of Atlantic Canada—the first time in decades that there were no centre-right MPs from that region. Harper was re-elected in Calgary Heritage, essentially a reconfigured version of his former riding.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hours after conceding defeat on election night, Harper resigned as leader of the Conservative Party and returned to the backbench.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Former Cabinet minister <a href="/wiki/Rona_Ambrose" title="Rona Ambrose">Rona Ambrose</a> was elected interim leader by the Conservative caucus, pending a formal leadership election.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> Harper resigned as prime minister during a meeting with Governor General David Johnston, who accepted the resignation, after which Johnston invited Trudeau to form a government on November 4, 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-thestar.com_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thestar.com-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> After Andrew Scheer resigned as Conservative leader in 2019, the <i>National Post</i> criticized Harper, by stating that he "lost in 2015 in a way that left his party struggling to make any sense at all, including on deficits."<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_and_economic_policy">Domestic and economic policy</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_the_Harper_government" class="mw-redirect" title="Domestic policy of the Harper government">Domestic policy of the Harper government</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Canadian_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_@_the_Royal_Ontario_Museum_in_Toronto.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Canadian_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%40_the_Royal_Ontario_Museum_in_Toronto.jpg/220px-Canadian_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%40_the_Royal_Ontario_Museum_in_Toronto.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Canadian_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%40_the_Royal_Ontario_Museum_in_Toronto.jpg/330px-Canadian_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%40_the_Royal_Ontario_Museum_in_Toronto.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Canadian_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%40_the_Royal_Ontario_Museum_in_Toronto.jpg/440px-Canadian_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%40_the_Royal_Ontario_Museum_in_Toronto.jpg 2x" data-file-width="951" data-file-height="720" /></a><figcaption>Harper appearing at a gala at the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Ontario_Museum" title="Royal Ontario Museum">Royal Ontario Museum</a> in <a href="/wiki/Toronto" title="Toronto">Toronto</a> to celebrate the discovery of <a href="/wiki/HMS_Erebus_(1826)" title="HMS Erebus (1826)">HMS&#160;<i>Erebus</i></a>, one of two ships wrecked during <a href="/wiki/John_Franklin" title="John Franklin">John Franklin</a>'s lost expedition.</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Constitutional_issues">Constitutional issues</span></h4>
<p>After sidestepping the political landmine for most of the first year of his time as prime minister, much as all the post-Charlottetown Accord prime ministers had done, Harper's hand was forced to reopen the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_sovereignty_movement" title="Quebec sovereignty movement">Quebec sovereignty</a> debate after the opposition Bloc Québécois were to introduce a motion in the House that called for recognition of Quebec as a "nation". On November 22, 2006, Harper introduced <a href="/wiki/Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois_nation_motion" title="Québécois nation motion">his own motion</a> to recognize that "the <a href="/wiki/French-Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="French-Canadian">Québécois</a> form a nation within a united Canada."<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Five days later, Harper's motion passed, with a margin of 266–16; all federalist parties, and the Bloc Québécois, supported it.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2004, Harper said "the Upper House remains a dumping ground for the favoured cronies of the prime minister".<sup id="cite_ref-senatestar_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-senatestar-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Between 2006 and 2008, by which time Harper was prime minister, he did not put any names to the governor general for appointment to the Senate, resulting in 16 Senate vacancies by the October 2008 election.<sup id="cite_ref-theglobeandmail1_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-theglobeandmail1-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> The one exception was <a href="/wiki/Michael_Fortier" title="Michael Fortier">Michael Fortier</a>. When Harper took office, he advised the governor general to appoint Michael Fortier to both the Senate and the <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Canada" title="Cabinet of Canada">Cabinet</a>, arguing the government needed <a href="/wiki/Political_representation" title="Political representation">representation</a> from the city of Montreal.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> Although there is a precedent for this action in Canadian history, the appointment led to criticism from opponents who claimed Harper was reneging on his push for an elected Senate. In 2008, Fortier gave up his Senate seat and sought election as an MP, but was defeated by a large margin by the incumbent Bloc Québécois MP.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the October 2008, election, Harper again named Senate reform as a priority.<sup id="cite_ref-theglobeandmail1_163-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-theglobeandmail1-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> By December, he recommended the appointment of 18 senators and, in 2009, provided an additional nine people for appointment as senators. Many of those appointed had close ties with the Conservative Party, including the campaign manager of the Conservative Party, <a href="/wiki/Doug_Finley" title="Doug Finley">Doug Finley</a>. Critics accused Harper of hypocrisy (the Liberals coined the term "Harpocrisy"). Conservative senator Bert Brown defended Harper's appointments and said "the only way [the Senate]'s ever been filled is by having people that are loyal to the prime minister who's appointing them".<sup id="cite_ref-senatestar_162-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-senatestar-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_management">Economic management</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stephen-Harper-January-26-2012.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Stephen-Harper-January-26-2012.png/150px-Stephen-Harper-January-26-2012.png" decoding="async" width="150" height="185" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Stephen-Harper-January-26-2012.png/225px-Stephen-Harper-January-26-2012.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Stephen-Harper-January-26-2012.png/300px-Stephen-Harper-January-26-2012.png 2x" data-file-width="407" data-file-height="501" /></a><figcaption>Harper during the 'Special Address' at the 2012 Annual Meeting of the <a href="/wiki/World_Economic_Forum" title="World Economic Forum">World Economic Forum</a> at the congress centre in Davos, Switzerland, January 26, 2012.</figcaption></figure>
<p>During Harper's tenure, Canada had budgetary surpluses in <a href="/wiki/2006_Canadian_federal_budget" title="2006 Canadian federal budget">2006</a> and <a href="/wiki/2007_Canadian_federal_budget" title="2007 Canadian federal budget">2007</a> of $13.8 and 9.6 billion respectively. Following the <a href="/wiki/Financial_crisis_of_2007%E2%80%932008" class="mw-redirect" title="Financial crisis of 2007–2008">2008 financial crisis</a>, Canada ran deficits from <a href="/wiki/2008_Canadian_federal_budget" title="2008 Canadian federal budget">2008</a>–<a href="/wiki/2013_Canadian_federal_budget" title="2013 Canadian federal budget">2013</a>. The deficit was $55.6 billion in <a href="/wiki/2009_Canadian_federal_budget" title="2009 Canadian federal budget">2009</a> and was gradually lowered to $5.2 billion in 2013. In <a href="/wiki/2014_Canadian_federal_budget" title="2014 Canadian federal budget">2014</a>, the federal budget was balanced with a surplus of $1.9 billion.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> For the first 11 months of the <a href="/wiki/2015_Canadian_federal_budget" title="2015 Canadian federal budget">2015–2016</a> period, the federal government was on track for a $7.5 billion surplus. For 2015–2016, the federal government projected a $1.4-billion surplus.<sup id="cite_ref-cbc.ca_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc.ca-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Following the 2015 federal election and a change in government, the 2015 fiscal year ended in a $1 billion deficit instead.<sup id="cite_ref-cbc.ca_167-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cbc.ca-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> In 2010, Canada had the lowest <a href="/wiki/Debt-to-GDP_ratio" title="Debt-to-GDP ratio">debt-to-GDP ratio</a> in the G7 economies.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> <i>The Economist</i> magazine stated that Canada had come out the recession stronger than any other rich country in the G7.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> In 2013, Canada came out with <a href="/wiki/Canada%27s_Global_Markets_Action_Plan" title="Canada&#39;s Global Markets Action Plan">Global Markets Action Plan</a> to generate employment opportunities for Canadians.<sup id="cite_ref-GMAP_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GMAP-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-TNFAVSF_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TNFAVSF-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-TIMFPTT_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TIMFPTT-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="2011_Census">2011 Census</span></h4>
<p>Ahead of the <a href="/wiki/Canada_2011_Census" class="mw-redirect" title="Canada 2011 Census">Canada 2011 Census</a>, the government announced that the long-form questionnaire (which collects detailed demographic information) will no longer be mandatory. According to <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Industry_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Industry (Canada)">Minister of Industry</a> Tony Clement, the change was made because of privacy-related complaints and after consulting with <a href="/wiki/Statistics_Canada" title="Statistics Canada">Statistics Canada</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> However, the federal <a href="/wiki/Privacy_Commissioner_of_Canada" title="Privacy Commissioner of Canada">privacy commissioner</a> reported only receiving three complaints between 1995 and 2010, according to a report in the <i>Toronto Sun</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Munir_Sheikh" title="Munir Sheikh">Munir Sheikh</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Chief_Statistician_of_Canada" title="Chief Statistician of Canada">chief statistician of Canada</a>—appointed on Harper's advice—resigned on July 21, 2010, in protest of the government's change in policy.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Ivan_Fellegi" title="Ivan Fellegi">Ivan Fellegi</a>, a former chief statistician, criticized the government's decision, saying that those who are most vulnerable (such as the poor, new immigrants, and aboriginals) are least likely to respond to a voluntary form, which weakens information about their demographic.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The move was opposed by some governmental and non-governmental organizations.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Federation_of_Canadian_Municipalities" title="Federation of Canadian Municipalities">Federation of Canadian Municipalities</a>; the <a href="/wiki/Municipal_government_of_Toronto" title="Municipal government of Toronto">Toronto government</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Jewish_Congress" title="Canadian Jewish Congress">Canadian Jewish Congress</a>; <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Fellowship_of_Canada" title="Evangelical Fellowship of Canada">Evangelical Fellowship of Canada</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Conference_of_Catholic_Bishops" title="Canadian Conference of Catholic Bishops">Canadian Conference of Catholic Bishops</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Medical_Association" title="Canadian Medical Association">Canadian Medical Association</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Statistical_Society_of_Canada" title="Statistical Society of Canada">Statistical Society of Canada</a>; the <a href="/wiki/American_Statistical_Association" title="American Statistical Association">American Statistical Association</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Registered_Nurses_Association_of_Ontario" class="mw-redirect" title="Registered Nurses Association of Ontario">Registered Nurses Association of Ontario</a> all opposed the change. However, the <a href="/wiki/Fraser_Institute" title="Fraser Institute">Fraser Institute</a> supported the change.<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> The provincial governments of <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Ontario" title="Government of Ontario">Ontario</a>, <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Quebec" title="Government of Quebec">Quebec</a>, <a href="/wiki/Government_of_New_Brunswick" title="Government of New Brunswick">New Brunswick</a>, <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Prince_Edward_Island" title="Government of Prince Edward Island">Prince Edward Island</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Manitoba" title="Government of Manitoba">Manitoba</a>, also opposed the change.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Veterans">Veterans</span></h4>
<p>Under Harper, the annual budget of <a href="/wiki/Veterans_Affairs_Canada" title="Veterans Affairs Canada">Veterans Affairs Canada</a> increased from $2.85 billion in 2005–2006 to $3.55 billion in 2014–2015, while the quantity of veterans served has declined from 219,152 in 2008–2009 to 199,154 in 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> Nine Veterans Affairs offices were closed between 2012 and 2015, and 900 positions were phased out from the department since 2009. Former-<a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Veterans_Affairs_(Canada)" title="Minister of Veterans Affairs (Canada)">minister of veterans affairs</a> <a href="/wiki/Erin_O%27Toole" title="Erin O&#39;Toole">Erin O'Toole</a> stated that the closures were made to modernize the department, by moving services online and to <a href="/wiki/Service_Canada" title="Service Canada">Service Canada</a> locations.<sup id="cite_ref-Closing_VA_offices_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Closing_VA_offices-189">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> In 2006, Harper implemented the New Veterans Charter passed with all party support by the previous Liberal government.<sup id="cite_ref-Veterans_spending_Globe_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Veterans_spending_Globe-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> This charter gave veterans the option to select a lump-sum payment, an annual installment over the number of years of a Veteran's choosing, or a combination of these two payment options.<sup id="cite_ref-New_Veterans_Charter_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-New_Veterans_Charter-191">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Myth_Busting_the_New_Veterans_Charter_Lump_Sum_Payment_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Myth_Busting_the_New_Veterans_Charter_Lump_Sum_Payment-192">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> Under Harper, the Canadian government spent $700,000 fighting a class-action lawsuit brought by a group of wounded Afghan veterans who argued that the new charter was discriminatory.<sup id="cite_ref-Fighting_veterans_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fighting_veterans-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Stephen_Harper_government" title="Foreign policy of the Stephen Harper government">Foreign policy of the Stephen Harper government</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cristina_Kirchner_and_Canada_PM_Stephen_Harper.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Cristina_Kirchner_and_Canada_PM_Stephen_Harper.jpg/220px-Cristina_Kirchner_and_Canada_PM_Stephen_Harper.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Cristina_Kirchner_and_Canada_PM_Stephen_Harper.jpg/330px-Cristina_Kirchner_and_Canada_PM_Stephen_Harper.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Cristina_Kirchner_and_Canada_PM_Stephen_Harper.jpg/440px-Cristina_Kirchner_and_Canada_PM_Stephen_Harper.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1333" /></a><figcaption>President of Argentina <a href="/wiki/Cristina_Fern%C3%A1ndez_de_Kirchner" title="Cristina Fernández de Kirchner">Cristina Kirchner</a> and Harper in <a href="/wiki/Toronto" title="Toronto">Toronto</a>, 2010.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:G7_summit_2015.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/G7_summit_2015.jpg/220px-G7_summit_2015.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/G7_summit_2015.jpg/330px-G7_summit_2015.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/G7_summit_2015.jpg/440px-G7_summit_2015.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5472" data-file-height="3648" /></a><figcaption>Harper at the <a href="/wiki/41st_G7_summit" title="41st G7 summit">2015 G-7 summit</a> with <a href="/wiki/Shinz%C5%8D_Abe" class="mw-redirect" title="Shinzō Abe">Shinzō Abe</a>, <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a>, <a href="/wiki/Angela_Merkel" title="Angela Merkel">Angela Merkel</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Hollande" title="François Hollande">François Hollande</a>, <a href="/wiki/David_Cameron" title="David Cameron">David Cameron</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Matteo_Renzi" title="Matteo Renzi">Matteo Renzi</a> in <a href="/wiki/Bavaria" title="Bavaria">Bavaria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>During his term, Harper dealt with many foreign policy issues relating to the United States, the <a href="/wiki/War_on_Terror" class="mw-redirect" title="War on Terror">War on Terror</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_conflict" title="Arab–Israeli conflict">Arab–Israeli conflict</a>, free trade, <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">China</a>, and Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He reduced defence spending to 1 per cent of Canadian GDP.<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2009, Harper visited China. During the visit Chinese Premier <a href="/wiki/Wen_Jiabao" title="Wen Jiabao">Wen Jiabao</a> publicly scolded Harper for not visiting earlier, pointing out that "this is the first meeting between the Chinese premier and a Canadian prime minister in almost five years"; Harper in response said that, "it's almost been five years since we had yourself or President <a href="/wiki/Hu_Jintao" title="Hu Jintao">Hu</a> in our country."<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> In 2008, former-prime minister <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a> had criticized Harper for missing opening ceremonies for the <a href="/wiki/2008_Summer_Olympics" title="2008 Summer Olympics">2008 Summer Olympics</a> in <a href="/wiki/Beijing" title="Beijing">Beijing</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-197">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> in response, <a href="/wiki/Dimitri_Soudas" title="Dimitri Soudas">Dimitri Soudas</a>, a spokesperson for Harper, called the remarks hypocritical, pointing out that Chrétien "attended one of six Olympic opening ceremonies during his 13&#32;&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Sic" title="Sic">sic</a></i>&#93; years as prime minister".<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On September 11, 2007, Harper visited Australia and addressed <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Australia" title="Parliament of Australia">its Parliament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On January 20, 2014, Harper addressed the Israeli Knesset in Givat Ram, Jerusalem.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During mid-2015, Harper repeatedly voiced his opinion that Russia should be excluded from association with the G7 group of nations because of Russia's support for Russian-speaking Ukrainian dissidents. On June 8, Harper said,"Mr. Putin ... has no place at the [G7] table, and I don't believe there's any leader who would defend Mr. Putin having a place."<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Michael_Ignatieff" title="Michael Ignatieff">Michael Ignatieff</a> criticized Harper for cutting foreign aid to Africa by $700 million, falling short of the <a href="/wiki/UN_Millennium_Development_Goals" class="mw-redirect" title="UN Millennium Development Goals">UN Millennium Development Goals</a>, and cutting eight African countries from the list of priority aid recipients.<sup id="cite_ref-Ignat_foreign_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ignat_foreign-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Afghanistan">Afghanistan</span></h4>
<p>On March 11 and 12, 2006, Harper made a surprise trip to <a href="/wiki/Afghanistan" title="Afghanistan">Afghanistan</a>, where Canadian Forces personnel <a href="/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%93present)" class="mw-redirect" title="War in Afghanistan (2001–present)">had been deployed</a> as part of the <a href="/wiki/NATO" title="NATO">NATO</a>-led <a href="/wiki/International_Security_Assistance_Force" title="International Security Assistance Force">International Security Assistance Force</a> since late 2001, to visit troops in theatre as a show of support for their efforts, and as a demonstration of the government's commitment to reconstruction and stability in the region. Harper's choice of a first foreign visit was closely guarded from the press until his arrival in Afghanistan (citing security concerns), and is seen as marking a significant change in relationship between the government and the military. Harper returned to Afghanistan on May 22, 2007, in a surprise two-day visit which included visiting Canadian troops at the forward operating base at Ma'Sum Ghar, located 25 kilometres (16&#160;mi) south of <a href="/wiki/Kandahar" title="Kandahar">Kandahar</a>, making Harper the first prime minister to have visited the front lines of a combat operation.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Israel">Israel</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_2006_Lebanon_War" title="International reactions to the 2006 Lebanon War">International reactions to the 2006 Lebanon War</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Toronto_protest_banner_criticising_Harper%27s_Israel-Lebanon_response.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Toronto_protest_banner_criticising_Harper%27s_Israel-Lebanon_response.jpg/220px-Toronto_protest_banner_criticising_Harper%27s_Israel-Lebanon_response.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Toronto_protest_banner_criticising_Harper%27s_Israel-Lebanon_response.jpg/330px-Toronto_protest_banner_criticising_Harper%27s_Israel-Lebanon_response.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Toronto_protest_banner_criticising_Harper%27s_Israel-Lebanon_response.jpg/440px-Toronto_protest_banner_criticising_Harper%27s_Israel-Lebanon_response.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a><figcaption>A banner criticizing Harper's response to the 2006 Israel–Lebanon conflict, Toronto.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Harper has shown admiration for <a href="/wiki/Israel" title="Israel">Israel</a> since the early 1990s. Friends and colleagues describe his views as being the product of thinking and reading deeply about the Middle East. Toronto rabbi Philip Scheim, who accompanied Harper to Israel in 2014 said, "I sense that [Harper] sees Israel as a manifestation of justice and a righting of historical wrongs, especially in light of <a href="/wiki/The_Holocaust" title="The Holocaust">the Holocaust</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the outset of the <a href="/wiki/2006_Israel%E2%80%93Lebanon_conflict" class="mw-redirect" title="2006 Israel–Lebanon conflict">2006 Israel–Lebanon conflict</a>, Harper defended Israel's "right to defend itself" and described its military campaign in Lebanon as a "measured" response, arguing that <a href="/wiki/Hezbollah" title="Hezbollah">Hezbollah</a>'s release of kidnapped <a href="/wiki/Israel_Defense_Force" class="mw-redirect" title="Israel Defense Force">Israel Defense Force</a> (IDF) soldiers would be the key to ending the conflict.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> Speaking of the situation in both Lebanon and <a href="/wiki/Gaza_Strip" title="Gaza Strip">Gaza</a> on July 18, Harper said he wanted "not just a ceasefire, but a resolution" but such a thing would not happen until Hezbollah and <a href="/wiki/Hamas" title="Hamas">Hamas</a> recognize Israel's right to exist. Harper blamed Hezbollah for all the civilian deaths. He asserted that Hezbollah's objective is to destroy Israel through violence.<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The media noted that Harper did not allow reporters opportunities to ask him questions on his position. Some Canadians, including many Arab and Lebanese Canadians, criticized Harper's description of Israel's response.<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 2008, the <a href="/wiki/Conference_of_Presidents_of_Major_American_Jewish_Organizations" title="Conference of Presidents of Major American Jewish Organizations">Conference of Presidents of Major American Jewish Organizations</a> recognized Harper's support for Israel with its inaugural International Leadership Award, pointing out Harper's decision to boycott the <a href="/wiki/Durban_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Durban II">Durban II</a> anti-racism conference, and his government's "support for Israel and [its] efforts at the U.N. against incitement and<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... the <a href="/wiki/Delegitimization_of_Israel" class="mw-redirect" title="Delegitimization of Israel">delegitimization of Israel</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 2009, Harper spoke at a <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Hill" title="Parliament Hill">Parliament Hill</a> ceremony organized by <a href="/wiki/Chabad-Lubavitch" class="mw-redirect" title="Chabad-Lubavitch">Chabad-Lubavitch</a> to honour the Jewish victims of the <a href="/wiki/2008_Mumbai_attacks" title="2008 Mumbai attacks">2008 Mumbai attacks</a>, which included an attack on the <a href="/wiki/Nariman_House" title="Nariman House">Nariman House</a>. He expressed condolences over the murder at Chabad's Mumbai centre of Rabbi <a href="/wiki/Gavriel_Holtzberg" title="Gavriel Holtzberg">Gavriel Holtzberg</a> and his wife Rivka. Harper described the killings as "affronts to the values that unite all civilized people". Harper added that the quick instalment of a new rabbi at the Chabad centre in Mumbai as a signal that the Jewish people will "never bow to violence and hatred".<sup id="cite_ref-pernicious_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pernicious-209">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2010, Canada lost a bid for a seat on the <a href="/wiki/UN_Security_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="UN Security Council">UN Security Council</a>. While initially blaming the loss on his rival Ignatieff, Harper later said that it was due to his pro-Israeli stance. Harper then said that he would take a pro-Israeli stance, no matter what the political cost to Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-CBC20101012_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CBC20101012-210">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-unseat_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-unseat-211">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> Ignatieff criticized Harper's stance as a "mistake", saying Canada would be better able to defend Israel through the Security Council than from the sidelines and pointed out that it is the Security Council that will determine if sanctions are imposed on <a href="/wiki/Iran" title="Iran">Iran</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-unseat_211-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-unseat-211">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> Ignatieff also accused Harper of steering the discussion away from implementing the <a href="/wiki/Two-state_solution" title="Two-state solution">two-state solution</a>, and instead of rendering all discussion into a competition "about who is Israel's best friend".<sup id="cite_ref-Ignat_foreign_202-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ignat_foreign-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper backed Israel's <a href="/wiki/2014_Israel%E2%80%93Gaza_conflict" class="mw-redirect" title="2014 Israel–Gaza conflict">2014 war in Gaza</a> and condemned Hamas. Harper said, "It is evident that Hamas is deliberately using human shields to further terror in the region."<sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Free_trade_with_EFTA">Free trade with EFTA</span></h4>
<p>On June 7, 2007, the Conservative government announced it had finalized free trade negotiations with the <a href="/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association" title="European Free Trade Association">European Free Trade Association</a> (EFTA). Under this agreement, Canada increased its trade ties with Iceland, Norway, Switzerland and <a href="/wiki/Liechtenstein" title="Liechtenstein">Liechtenstein</a>. In 2006, the value of trade between these partners was $10.7 billion. Canada had originally begun negotiations with the EFTA on October 9, 1998, but talks broke down because of a disagreement over subsidies to shipyards in Atlantic Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="United_States">United States</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_relations" title="Canada–United States relations">Canada–United States relations</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Barack_Obama_meets_Stephen_Harper.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Barack_Obama_meets_Stephen_Harper.jpg/220px-Barack_Obama_meets_Stephen_Harper.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Barack_Obama_meets_Stephen_Harper.jpg/330px-Barack_Obama_meets_Stephen_Harper.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Barack_Obama_meets_Stephen_Harper.jpg/440px-Barack_Obama_meets_Stephen_Harper.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="682" /></a><figcaption>Harper meets with US President <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a> in Ottawa, February 19, 2009.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Secretary_of_State_Hillary_Clinton_and_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_(4308896411).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Secretary_of_State_Hillary_Clinton_and_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%284308896411%29.jpg/220px-Secretary_of_State_Hillary_Clinton_and_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%284308896411%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="174" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Secretary_of_State_Hillary_Clinton_and_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%284308896411%29.jpg/330px-Secretary_of_State_Hillary_Clinton_and_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%284308896411%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Secretary_of_State_Hillary_Clinton_and_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%284308896411%29.jpg/440px-Secretary_of_State_Hillary_Clinton_and_Prime_Minister_Stephen_Harper_%284308896411%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3067" data-file-height="2422" /></a><figcaption>Harper and <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">US Secretary of State</a> <a href="/wiki/Hillary_Clinton" title="Hillary Clinton">Hillary Clinton</a> at the Haiti Ministerial Preparatory Conference addressing <a href="/wiki/Humanitarian_response_to_the_2010_Haiti_earthquake" title="Humanitarian response to the 2010 Haiti earthquake">earthquake relief</a> in <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>, January 25, 2010.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Shortly after being congratulated by George W. Bush for his victory, Harper rebuked US Ambassador <a href="/wiki/David_H._Wilkins" title="David H. Wilkins">David Wilkins</a> for criticizing the Conservatives' plans to assert Canada's sovereignty over the <a href="/wiki/Arctic_Ocean" title="Arctic Ocean">Arctic Ocean</a> waters with armed forces.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> Harper's first meeting as prime minister with the US president occurred at the end of March 2006.
</p><p>The government received American news coverage during the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)_presidential_primaries,_2008" class="mw-redirect" title="Democratic Party (United States) presidential primaries, 2008">Democratic Party's 2008 presidential primaries</a> after the details of a conversation between Barack Obama's economic advisor <a href="/wiki/Austan_Goolsbee" title="Austan Goolsbee">Austan Goolsbee</a>, and Canadian diplomat Georges Rioux were revealed. Reportedly Goolsbee was reassuring the Canadians that Obama's comments on potentially renegotiating the North American Free Trade Agreement (NAFTA) were more political rhetoric than actual policy. The accuracy of these reports has been debated by both the Obama campaign and the Canadian government. The news came at a key time nearing the <a href="/wiki/Ohio_Democratic_primary,_2008" class="mw-redirect" title="Ohio Democratic primary, 2008">Ohio</a> and <a href="/wiki/Texas_Democratic_primary_and_caucuses,_2008" class="mw-redirect" title="Texas Democratic primary and caucuses, 2008">Texas</a> primaries, where perceptions among <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">Democratic</a> voters was (and is) that the benefits of the NAFTA agreement are dubious. Thus the appearance that Obama was not being completely forthright was attacked by his opponent Hillary Clinton.<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/ABC_News" title="ABC News">ABC News</a> reported that Harper's <a href="/wiki/Chief_of_Staff_to_the_Prime_Minister_(Canada)" title="Chief of Staff to the Prime Minister (Canada)">chief of staff</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ian_Brodie" title="Ian Brodie">Ian Brodie</a> was responsible for the details reaching the hands of the media.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> Harper has denied that Brodie was responsible for the leak, and launched an investigation to find the source. The <a href="/wiki/Opposition_(parliamentary)" class="mw-redirect" title="Opposition (parliamentary)">Opposition</a>, as well as Democratic strategist <a href="/wiki/Bob_Shrum" title="Bob Shrum">Bob Shrum</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> criticized the government on the issue, stating they were trying to help the <a href="/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)" title="Republican Party (United States)">Republicans</a> by helping Hillary Clinton win the Democratic nomination instead of Obama. They also alleged the leak would hurt relations with the United States if Obama ever were to become President.<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> Obama was elected president in November. In February, Obama made his first foreign visit as the US president to Ottawa, in which he affirmed support for free trade with Canada, as well as complimenting Canada on its involvement in Afghanistan.<sup id="cite_ref-220" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-220">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Environmental_policy">Environmental policy</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Environmental_policy_of_the_Stephen_Harper_government" title="Environmental policy of the Stephen Harper government">Environmental policy of the Stephen Harper government</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stephen_Harper_@_Vancouver_Island_University%27s_Deep_Bay_Marine_Field_Station.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Stephen_Harper_%40_Vancouver_Island_University%27s_Deep_Bay_Marine_Field_Station.jpg/220px-Stephen_Harper_%40_Vancouver_Island_University%27s_Deep_Bay_Marine_Field_Station.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="143" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Stephen_Harper_%40_Vancouver_Island_University%27s_Deep_Bay_Marine_Field_Station.jpg/330px-Stephen_Harper_%40_Vancouver_Island_University%27s_Deep_Bay_Marine_Field_Station.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Stephen_Harper_%40_Vancouver_Island_University%27s_Deep_Bay_Marine_Field_Station.jpg/440px-Stephen_Harper_%40_Vancouver_Island_University%27s_Deep_Bay_Marine_Field_Station.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1641" data-file-height="1064" /></a><figcaption>Stephen Harper visiting <a href="/wiki/Vancouver_Island_University" title="Vancouver Island University">Vancouver Island University</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Deep_Bay_Marine_Field_Station" title="Deep Bay Marine Field Station">Deep Bay Marine Field Station</a> in 2010.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Since Harper's government took office in 2006, Canadian greenhouse gas emissions fell from 749 to 726&#160;Mt of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO<sub>2</sub> eq). From 1993 until 2006, during the previous Liberal government greenhouse gas emissions increased from 600 to 749&#160;Mt of CO<sub>2</sub> eq.<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> The reduction corresponded Canada's decreased economic output during the <a href="/wiki/Great_Recession" title="Great Recession">Great Recession</a> and emissions began increasing slightly in 2010, when the economy began recovering.<sup id="cite_ref-Reality_check_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reality_check-222">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> Other significant factors in Canada's decreased emissions during Harper's tenure are initiatives such as the <a href="/wiki/Carbon_tax" title="Carbon tax">carbon tax</a> in British Columbia, the <a href="/wiki/Cap_and_trade" class="mw-redirect" title="Cap and trade">cap and trade</a> system in Quebec, Ontario discontinuing use of <a href="/wiki/Coal-fired_power_plant" class="mw-redirect" title="Coal-fired power plant">coal-fired power plants</a>, and the Clean Air Regulatory Agenda which regulates emissions for automobiles and light trucks.<sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Lower_Churchill_Project_Loan_Guarantee_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lower_Churchill_Project_Loan_Guarantee-225">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> In 2006, a Clean Air and Climate Change Act was proposed to address air pollution as well as greenhouse gas emissions; it never became law.<sup id="cite_ref-Green_Lies_226-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Green_Lies-226">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> In 2006, the Clean Air Regulatory Agenda (CARA) was established to "support Government of Canada efforts to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) and air pollutant emissions in order to improve the environment and health of Canadians".<sup id="cite_ref-Clean_Air_Regulatory_Agenda_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clean_Air_Regulatory_Agenda-228">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> In December 2011, the Harper government announced that Canada would formally withdraw from the <a href="/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol" title="Kyoto Protocol">Kyoto Protocol</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Canada_abandons_Kyoto_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Canada_abandons_Kyoto-229">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> Environment Minister <a href="/wiki/Peter_Kent" title="Peter Kent">Peter Kent</a> stated, "It's now clear that Kyoto is not the path forward for a global solution to climate change."<sup id="cite_ref-Canada_abandons_Kyoto_229-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Canada_abandons_Kyoto-229">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> In December 2012, Canada became the first signatory to formally withdraw from the Kyoto Protocol.<sup id="cite_ref-Postmedia-withdrawal_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Postmedia-withdrawal-230">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Renewable_energy">Renewable energy</span></h4>
<p>Other federal initiatives include the 2011 loan guarantee towards the <a href="/wiki/Lower_Churchill_Project" class="mw-redirect" title="Lower Churchill Project">Lower Churchill Project</a> in <a href="/wiki/Labrador" title="Labrador">Labrador</a>, which is scheduled for completion in 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-Lower_Churchill_Project:_Ottawa_Guarantees_$6.2-Billion_Hydroelectric_Project_On_Muskrat_Falls_For_Newfoundland_And_Labrador_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lower_Churchill_Project:_Ottawa_Guarantees_$6.2-Billion_Hydroelectric_Project_On_Muskrat_Falls_For_Newfoundland_And_Labrador-232">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> The Lower Churchill's two hydroelectric installations at <a href="/wiki/Gull_Island,_Labrador" title="Gull Island, Labrador">Gull Island</a> and <a href="/wiki/Muskrat_Falls" title="Muskrat Falls">Muskrat Falls</a> will have a combined capacity of over 3,074&#160;MW and have the ability to provide 16.7&#160;TW·h of electricity per year, which is enough to "reduce greenhouse gas emissions equivalent to taking 3.2 million vehicles off the road each year".<sup id="cite_ref-Lower_Churchill_Project_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lower_Churchill_Project-233">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Harper_pledges_to_support_major_$6.2_billion_hydroelectric_project_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harper_pledges_to_support_major_$6.2_billion_hydroelectric_project-234">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Public_transit">Public transit</span></h4>
<p>In 2006, Harper introduced a Public Transit Tax Credit, where individuals could claim 15 per cent of the cost of a transit pass each year.<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup> From 2006 to 2013, the Harper government invested over $5 billion towards public transit projects in Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-Minister_Denis_Lebel_confirms_Harper_Government&#39;s_record_infrastructure_investment_will_boost_Canadian_communities_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Minister_Denis_Lebel_confirms_Harper_Government&#39;s_record_infrastructure_investment_will_boost_Canadian_communities-236">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-On_the_Right_Track_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-On_the_Right_Track-237">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup> In 2006, the federal government provided $697 million towards the <a href="/wiki/Line_1_Yonge%E2%80%93University#Toronto–York_Spadina_Subway_Extension" title="Line 1 Yonge–University">Toronto York–Spadina Subway Extension</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-238" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-238">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup> In September 2013, Finance Minister <a href="/wiki/Jim_Flaherty" title="Jim Flaherty">Jim Flaherty</a> announced a federal contribution of $660 million towards the <a href="/wiki/Scarborough_Subway_Extension" class="mw-redirect" title="Scarborough Subway Extension">Scarborough Subway Extension</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Harper_pledges_federal_funding_for_Toronto&#39;s_subway_extension_239-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harper_pledges_federal_funding_for_Toronto&#39;s_subway_extension-239">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ottawa_will_help_pay_for_Scarborough_subway_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ottawa_will_help_pay_for_Scarborough_subway-240">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Major_transit_announcement_incoming:_Harper_expected_to_boost_TTC_funding_during_Toronto_visit_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Major_transit_announcement_incoming:_Harper_expected_to_boost_TTC_funding_during_Toronto_visit-241">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transparency">Transparency</span></h3>
<p>Starting in 2006, the Harper government implemented policies that had the effect of reducing transparency. During this government, scientists employed by the government were not able to speak with the media and inform the public of their findings without government permission,<sup id="cite_ref-:3_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-242">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:4_243-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-243">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:5_244-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-244">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:6_245-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-245">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup> the government made significant cuts to research and other forms of data collection,<sup id="cite_ref-:1_174-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:7_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-246">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> and significant destruction and inaccessibility of government-held data and documents occurred.<sup id="cite_ref-:8_247-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:8-247">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-248" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-248">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the Harper government, it was not possible for government-employed scientists to openly speak about the government policy that prohibited communication with the media. However, following the election of a new government in 2015, several scientists who were or had been employed by the government came forward to confirm the allegations made by anonymous sources during the Harper years.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_242-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-242">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:4_243-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-243">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:5_244-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-244">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:6_245-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-245">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The government made drastic cuts to scientific research and data collection. Over 2,000 scientists were dismissed and funding was cut from world renowned research facilities. Cuts were also made to many essential programs, some so deep that they had to shut down entirely, including the monitoring of smoke stack emissions, food inspections, oil spills, water quality, and climate change.<sup id="cite_ref-:7_246-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-246">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> During this time, the long form census was also discontinued as a mandatory part of the census. This was ostensibly due to privacy concerns, however, the number of complaints about privacy proved to be minimal.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_175-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The government closed a number of government libraries without consultation on the closings or the process involved. The manner in which it was done received significant criticism because it left the remaining information in disarray, inaccessible for research.<sup id="cite_ref-:7_246-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-246">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:8_247-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:8-247">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Supreme_Court_appointments">Supreme Court appointments</span></h2>
<p>Harper chose the following jurists to be appointed as justices of the Supreme Court of Canada:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Marshall_Rothstein" title="Marshall Rothstein">Marshall Rothstein</a> (March 1, 2006 – August 31, 2015)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Thomas_Cromwell_(jurist)" title="Thomas Cromwell (jurist)">Thomas Cromwell</a> (September 5, 2008 – September 1, 2016)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Andromache_Karakatsanis" title="Andromache Karakatsanis">Andromache Karakatsanis</a> (October 21, 2011 – present)<sup id="cite_ref-SecretProcess_249-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SecretProcess-249">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Michael_J._Moldaver" class="mw-redirect" title="Michael J. Moldaver">Michael J. Moldaver</a> (October 21, 2011 – September 1, 2022)<sup id="cite_ref-SecretProcess_249-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SecretProcess-249">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Richard_Wagner_(judge)" title="Richard Wagner (judge)">Richard Wagner</a> (October 5, 2012 – present)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Cl%C3%A9ment_Gascon" title="Clément Gascon">Clément Gascon</a> (June 9, 2014 – September 15, 2019)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Suzanne_C%C3%B4t%C3%A9" title="Suzanne Côté">Suzanne Côté</a> (December 1, 2014 – present)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Russell_Brown_(judge)" title="Russell Brown (judge)">Russell Brown</a> (August 31, 2015 – June 12, 2023)</li></ul>
<h2><span id="Conservative_backbencher_.282015.E2.80.932016.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Conservative_backbencher_(2015–2016)">Conservative backbencher (2015–2016)</span></h2>
<p>Harper returned to Ottawa as a Conservative backbencher and addressed a meeting of the Conservative caucus that included defeated MPs in November 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-Low_profile_250-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Low_profile-250">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> Interim leader Rona Ambrose stated that Harper would be in the House for key votes as the member for Calgary Heritage, but had earned the right to keep a low profile after his service as the prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-Low_profile_250-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Low_profile-250">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> In February 2018, Harper stated that he could have still "easily" been leader of the Conservative Party, but he chose not to amass too much power in order to secure the party's fortunes in the future.<sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 2015, Harper had set up Harper &amp; Associates Consulting Inc., a corporation that lists him a director alongside close associates <a href="/wiki/Ray_Novak" title="Ray Novak">Ray Novak</a> and Jeremy Hunt.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_252-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-252">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Harper announced in May 2016 that he planned to resign his seat in the House of Commons during the summer before the fall session of Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-253" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-253">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> On May 26, 2016, he was named as a board member for the Conservative Party's fundraising arm.<sup id="cite_ref-:9_254-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:9-254">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> The Conservative Fund is noted to have influence on the party operations.<sup id="cite_ref-:10_255-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:10-255">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> Harper and other directors played a role in the removal of Harper–appointed Conservative executive director Dustin Van Vught to avoid backlash from donors and grassroots conservatives.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:10_255-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:10-255">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> In the same month, Harper delivered a speech to the 2016 Conservative Party convention where his accomplishments as party leader and prime minister were honoured by the party.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_252-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-252">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="After_politics_.282016.E2.80.93present.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="After_politics_(2016–present)">After politics (2016–present)</span></h2>
<p>In October 2017, Harper received media attention for criticizing Prime Minister Justin Trudeau's handling of the renegotiation of the <a href="/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="North American Free Trade Agreement">North American Free Trade Agreement</a> started by the United States under President <a href="/wiki/Donald_Trump" title="Donald Trump">Donald Trump</a>, stating that Trudeau was too unwilling to make concessions to the U.S., sided too closely with Mexico, and tried to advance left-wing policies through the renegotiations.<sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-258" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-258">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On February 2, 2018, Harper revealed in a statement that he knew about the <a href="/wiki/Sexual_assault" title="Sexual assault">sexual assault</a> allegations against then Conservative MP <a href="/wiki/Rick_Dykstra" title="Rick Dykstra">Rick Dykstra</a> during the <a href="/wiki/2015_Canadian_federal_election" title="2015 Canadian federal election">2015 election</a> but could not justify removing him as a candidate because the investigation was closed by police a year before the election.<sup id="cite_ref-259" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-259">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_former_Prime_Minister_of_Canada,_Mr._Stephen_Harper_calls_on_the_Prime_Minister,_Shri_Narendra_Modi,_in_New_Delhi_on_January_15,_2018.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/The_former_Prime_Minister_of_Canada%2C_Mr._Stephen_Harper_calls_on_the_Prime_Minister%2C_Shri_Narendra_Modi%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_15%2C_2018.jpg/220px-The_former_Prime_Minister_of_Canada%2C_Mr._Stephen_Harper_calls_on_the_Prime_Minister%2C_Shri_Narendra_Modi%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_15%2C_2018.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="129" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/The_former_Prime_Minister_of_Canada%2C_Mr._Stephen_Harper_calls_on_the_Prime_Minister%2C_Shri_Narendra_Modi%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_15%2C_2018.jpg/330px-The_former_Prime_Minister_of_Canada%2C_Mr._Stephen_Harper_calls_on_the_Prime_Minister%2C_Shri_Narendra_Modi%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_15%2C_2018.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/The_former_Prime_Minister_of_Canada%2C_Mr._Stephen_Harper_calls_on_the_Prime_Minister%2C_Shri_Narendra_Modi%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_15%2C_2018.jpg/440px-The_former_Prime_Minister_of_Canada%2C_Mr._Stephen_Harper_calls_on_the_Prime_Minister%2C_Shri_Narendra_Modi%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_15%2C_2018.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2200" data-file-height="1290" /></a><figcaption>Harper with <a href="/wiki/Narendra_Modi" title="Narendra Modi">Narendra Modi</a> in India in 2018.</figcaption></figure>
<p>On March 26, 2018, Harper attended the international Fellowship of Christians and Jews Gala at <a href="/wiki/Mar-a-Lago" title="Mar-a-Lago">Mar-a-Lago</a> where he stated that he expressed support for US President Donald Trump's speech on <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-260" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-260">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> On May 9, he expressed support for Trump's decision to withdraw from the <a href="/wiki/Iran_deal" class="mw-redirect" title="Iran deal">Iran deal</a> by lending his signature to an ad that appeared in <i>The New York Times</i> a day after the decision.<sup id="cite_ref-261" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-261">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-262" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-262">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On November 19, 2018, Harper appeared on a show hosted by <a href="/wiki/Ben_Shapiro" title="Ben Shapiro">Ben Shapiro</a>, where he made comments on issues such as populism, immigration and nationalism. The <i>National Post</i> noted that they "echo the argument made in his recently released book, <i><a href="/wiki/Right_Here,_Right_Now_(book)" title="Right Here, Right Now (book)">Right Here, Right Now: Politics and Leadership in the Age of Disruption</a></i>, which urges conservatives to listen to populist grievances, rather than focus on other priorities like tax cuts for the wealthy."<sup id="cite_ref-263" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-263">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 2019, Harper appeared on a <a href="/wiki/PragerU" title="PragerU">PragerU</a> video explaining why Donald Trump was <a href="/wiki/Donald_Trump_2016_presidential_campaign" title="Donald Trump 2016 presidential campaign">elected to the presidency</a> in the <a href="/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election" title="2016 United States presidential election">2016 United States election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-265" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-265">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup> Then in May 2019, he appeared on another PragerU video explaining reasons to support Israel amid the <a href="/wiki/Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_conflict" title="Israeli–Palestinian conflict">Israeli–Palestinian conflict</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-266" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-266">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On March 11, 2021, during a <a href="/wiki/Web_conferencing" title="Web conferencing">virtual gathering</a> hosted by the Conference of Defence Associations Institute, Harper claimed that the world has entered into a <a href="/wiki/Second_Cold_War" title="Second Cold War">Second Cold War</a> between the United States and China, and that middle-power countries such as Canada are also a part of the rivalry between the two main powers.<sup id="cite_ref-267" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-267">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2021, Harper appeared on the podcast American Optimist, hosted by Joe Lonsdale. In the interview, Harper criticized the Trudeau government's large-scale <a href="/wiki/Deficit_spending" title="Deficit spending">deficit spending</a> during the <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Canada" title="COVID-19 pandemic in Canada">COVID-19 pandemic</a>, calling it "overkill". He also criticized "<a href="/wiki/Woke" title="Woke">woke</a> culture".<sup id="cite_ref-268" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-268">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On July 25, 2022, Harper endorsed his former cabinet minister <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Poilievre" title="Pierre Poilievre">Pierre Poilievre</a> for the <a href="/wiki/2022_Conservative_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="2022 Conservative Party of Canada leadership election">leadership of the Conservative Party</a>. This was the first time Harper endorsed a federal Conservative leadership candidate; he previously chose not to in the <a href="/wiki/2017_Conservative_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="2017 Conservative Party of Canada leadership election">2017</a> and <a href="/wiki/2020_Conservative_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="2020 Conservative Party of Canada leadership election">2020</a> leadership elections.<sup id="cite_ref-269" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-269">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 2022, Harper attended Queen Elizabeth II's <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Elizabeth_II" title="Death and state funeral of Elizabeth II">state funeral</a>, along with other former Canadian prime ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-270" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-270">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On March 22, 2023, during a conservative conference in Ottawa, Harper criticized the Trudeau government's handling of <a href="/wiki/Chinese_government_interference_in_the_2019_and_2021_Canadian_federal_elections" class="mw-redirect" title="Chinese government interference in the 2019 and 2021 Canadian federal elections">Chinese government interference in the 2019 and 2021 Canadian federal elections</a>. He also criticized the NDP, calling them a "branch plant of the Liberal party" and argued that the party got "nothing" out of its <a href="/wiki/Confidence-and-supply" class="mw-redirect" title="Confidence-and-supply">confidence-and-supply</a> agreement with the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-271" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-271">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 2023, Harper endorsed Albertan Premier <a href="/wiki/Danielle_Smith" title="Danielle Smith">Danielle Smith</a> for the <a href="/wiki/2023_Alberta_general_election" title="2023 Alberta general election">2023 Albertan provincial election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-272" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-272">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span></h2>

</p>
